<summary>This page is an archival cover sheet for manuscript No. 3053, identifying the work as the "Siddhanta Sarvabhauma," a significant Sanskrit text on mathematical astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Siddhanta Sarvabhauma, Munishvara, astronomy, archival label, Jammu, Shri Raghunath Temple Library</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal text of the Siddhānta Sārvabhauma, specifically the section on the sphere (Golādhyāya). It follows the archival cover page (No. 3053) previously translated. The author, Munīśvara, begins with a traditional invocation and then transitions from the mathematical calculations of planetary positions to the physical nature of the Earth and the heavens.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>
<header>Si. Sa. Gau. <note>An abbreviation for Siddhānta Sārvabhauma Golādhyāya (The Universal System of Principles: Chapter on the Sphere)</note></header>

**Om! Salutations to the illustrious Ganesha.** <note>The traditional elephant-headed deity invoked at the start of Hindu manuscripts to remove obstacles.</note>

Having supported all beings, the breath of the three worlds, full and supreme, the Lord of the Earth, whose form is the universe, who is unbroken consciousness, the natural and eternal bliss, the source of all the worlds—though they be illusory—to Him I bow. 1

In the Chapter on the Mean Motions of the Planets <note>original: Madhyādhikāra; the first section of a Siddhānta focusing on the mathematical average positions of planets</note>, the terrestrial sphere was mentioned. Here, I shall explain its true nature and position for the benefit of my students. Therefore, I speak now of the globe of the Earth. 2

From the illustrious Vasudeva <note>a name for the Supreme Divine</note> arose the soul-self, Sankarshana; from him, by his will, came the being Pradyumna, who is like a lamp of knowledge in the darkness. From him was produced Aniruddha. 3

From him came <term>Hiranyagarbha</term> <note>literally "the Golden Womb," referring to the Creator-God Brahma</note>, the dispeller of the darkness of great ignorance, the leader of all, the first being. He is the visible Sun, the extra-ordinary one, well-known in the Vedas, whose light is unhindered. 4

From him, by the power of his will, the world was created; the ego-driven Creator <note>original: Virañci; another name for Brahma</note> came into being. From his eyes came the Sun, from his heart the Moon, and from his feet and breath, the physical forms were obtained. 5

The planets, beginning with Mars, and the stars are all created as spheres. 

<margin>20</margin> <note>This marginal note likely refers to a verse number or a division of the 20 parts</note> The <term>Ecliptic</term> <note>original: krāntivṛtta; the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note> is divided into twelve parts <note>the Zodiac signs</note> and twenty-seven parts <note>the Lunar Mansions or Nakshatras</note>. 6

The atmospheric sphere <note>original: vāyu golā</note> was then created, along with the sky called <term>Pravaha</term> <note>original: pravahākhya; the "provector" wind believed in ancient Indian astronomy to drive the motion of the stars and planets</note>. Everything described here was arranged by <gloss>the Creator</gloss> <note>original: Brahmaṇā</note>. 7

The Earth is a composite of vapor, fire, and water. Because of their unity, the parts are equal. Together, they form the nature of the terrestrial globe, situated in the center of the cosmic womb. 8

The Creator made everything perfectly balanced. Because of its sphericity, it is called a "Gola" (sphere). Therefore, this globe is the support of all things and remains stable. 8

But this idea of it being "below" is only a matter of common perception. 9 <note>The author is beginning to argue that "up" and "down" are relative to the center of the sphere.</note>

If the Earth, being a massive object, required a physical supporter, there would be no end to the chain of supporters, leading to an infinite regress. Therefore, its stability is a natural property. In the works of <term>Bhaskara</term> <note>referring to Bhaskara II, the famous 12th-century astronomer</note>, it is said that the Earth stands by its own power, like a magnet, and this is logical. 10

The "Supporters of the Earth" mentioned in the <term>Puranas</term> <note>ancient Hindu texts of myth and lore</note>, such as the Great Serpent <margin>Ananta</margin> and others—what is the fault in them? They are mentioned in the Vedas. However, if the Earth were not moving, then why would its properties not be evident everywhere? 11 <note>The author is reconciling scriptural descriptions of the serpent Shesha holding the earth with physical observation.</note>

It is not as if a specific part is restricted; rather, the power of a physical mass is effective everywhere. Just as a pot or similar object does not have the power to draw water on its own, here too, one must understand the specific cause. 12

Even if one accepts the criticisms against it, why did Bhaskara mention this for the sake of explaining the world's stability? To protect the theory, he spoke of a "Stable Power" <note>original: sthira śakti</note>, which is indeed the Power of <term>Attraction</term> <note>original: ākarṣaṇā; a significant early reference to a concept similar to gravity</note>. Such is that power.

<margin>Birth</margin> 13 <note>The margin note "ja" probably starts the word "janma" (birth/origin)</note> 

Just as there is an attraction of water or an attraction of the stars, there is a delay in attraction because it is not seen here as great; it is seen as the opposite of that, due to the nature of the distance. 2

<summary>This page introduces the "Golādhyāya" section, beginning with a cosmological creation story and transitioning into a scientific discussion on the Earth's sphericity and its inherent "power of attraction" (gravity) to explain its stability in space without a physical supporter.</summary>
<keywords>Siddhanta Sarvabhauma, Munishvara, Bhaskara, astronomy, gravity, Akarshana, Earth's sphericity, Puranas, Hiranyagarbha, Pravaha wind</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues Munīśvara’s Siddhānta Sārvabhauma, moving from the cosmological origins described on the previous page to the physical proofs of the Earth’s spherical nature and its geographic layout. The text transitions into the Golādhyāya (Chapter on the Sphere), specifically addressing the "True Nature of the Earth" (Bhū-svarūpa).</meta>

...as stated regarding its position: 14. It attracts from the transverse and lower regions; in the same way, it acts from the upper regions. Because of this balance, it remains in the same place in space. 15. Indeed, because people at different times and in different places observe the stars and the planets at different elevations on the Earth, and because the directions like North appear different based on one's location, the Earth must be understood as being like a globe. 16. The distance between two observers is known as the interval; by the strength of the latitude and the displacement, the distance is determined. Furthermore, what is the use of mere words? 17. The measurement of the circumference has been calculated and explained. To those who know mathematics, the circumference above Lanka <note>In ancient Indian astronomy, Lanka refers to a theoretical point on the equator used as the prime meridian.</note> is two units. The distance between there and the observer is the "arrow" <note>original: śara; a geometric term referring to the versed sine, used here to calculate distances on a curved surface</note>...
<margin>Earth</margin>
...this is the measure of the Earth; it is not proper to consider it larger than this. 18. A tiny portion—specifically the 1/40th part—of the entire circumference appears even and flat to someone standing upon its surface. Therefore, the Earth seems like a flat circular surface to the observer. 19. Wherever a person stands, they consider themselves to be "on top" and think of the Earth as being "below" them. Therefore, even though this Earth is a globe, it appears to be a flat surface to those situated upon it. 20. Like reflections in water, those situated on the opposite side of the globe appear to be "head-downward." Yet, just as we feel we are on top, they too feel they are situated normally. 21.

Therefore, the Earth-globe <note>original: Ku-gola</note>, with its mountains, cities, hermitages, rivers, oceans, gods, demons, and humans, is covered on all sides, just as the <term>Kadamba flower</term> <note>The Kadamba (Neolamarckia cadamba) is a flowering tree; its spherical orange blossoms are covered in tiny projecting filaments, which Indian astronomers used as a perfect analogy for how things stick to the surface of the round Earth.</note> is covered by its own filaments. 22.

Aside from the regions of self-balance and the northern lands, there are four other divisions. These are separated by intervals of 1/4th parts, extending from the shores of the ocean toward the north. 23. The island of Meru <note>The central axis or mountain of the world in traditional cosmology</note> is in the middle of the Earth-globe. Regarding the southern regions, the arrangement of the islands and their names are known through observation. 24. From the equator, the regions are determined by dividing the area into 90 degrees. There, the divisions are explained through the expanse of the stars. 25. Then, the width of the island of Shaka <note>Shaka-dvīpa</note> is said to be one-third the measure of the Moon. From there, the wise who live by the light of the Moon say the following regions are increased by one-third parts. 26. Beyond that is the Shalmali island, shaped like a ring, and then the ocean. After that, by three-and-a-half parts, comes the island named Kusha. 27. Then, in a ring-like shape...

<summary>This page provides physical and mathematical arguments for the Earth's sphericity, explaining gravity as a balanced attraction and using the Kadamba flower analogy to describe life on a globe. It also begins a geographic description of the world's "islands" or continents (Dvīpas) based on astronomical measurements.</summary>

<keywords>Sphericity of Earth, Gravity, Lanka, Kadamba flower analogy, Antipodes, Shaka-dvīpa, Shalmali-dvīpa, Kusha-dvīpa, Indian Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the Earth's spherical nature. Having established that the Earth is a globe, Munīśvara now describes the concentric geography of the world—the traditional "islands" (dvīpas) and oceans—and transitions into a proto-scientific explanation of atmospheric phenomena like lightning, thunder, and rainbows, linking them to the heat of the subterranean fire and the movement of winds.</meta>

From the work *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma*: There is a distance from the prime meridian; then, by the measures of the arrows <note>original: śara; here referring to mathematical sine-segments used to calculate distances on the sphere</note> along the mountains, one reaches the island of Krauñca <note>A mythological continent in Hindu cosmology, often described as a ring surrounding the Earth's center</note>.
<margin>2</margin>
On the globe, at 28 parts, there is a ring-shaped northern table or path. From there, at a distance of six degrees, lies the island of Gomedaka.
From there, at a distance of the "good parts," lies the island of Puṣkara <note>The seventh and outermost of the concentric islands in Puranic geography</note>, located at 29 degrees. At the remaining part of the globe, at the southern pole, is the Vaḍavā <note>term: Vaḍavā/Vaḍavāgni; literally "mare's fire," a mythical submarine fire located at the South Pole in ancient Indian geography</note>.
At that central point, the ocean resembles an umbrella held transversely. Because of the sun’s position, it is surrounded by the sea. 30.
<margin>2</margin>
By five degrees is the region of the Vaḍavā fire. There, the messengers of the gods do not go; it is there that the demons and the residents of the hells <note>original: naraka</note> exist. 31.
In the middle of that ocean's water, the Submarine Fire <note>original: vāḍavāgni</note> is situated. From it, masses of smoke arise, submerged in the weight of the water. These are carried by the wind into the sky.
Led by the wind in all directions, they are heated by the rays of the sun <note>original: kumāri-kiraṇa; likely a reference to the sun's rays reaching the southern regions</note> and become lightning <note>term: vidyut; lightning</note>, which are like sparks of fire. 32.
From the slight burning of the smoke and the touch of the wind, the sun creates the sound of thunder <note>term: garjita; thunder</note>. This thunder is not exclusive to the rainy season <note>term: prāvṛṣi; the monsoon season</note>. 33.
For, while some clouds are full of rain, others elsewhere are "water-givers" with very little water; being filled with wind, they produce a great deal of thunder. 34.
Suddenly, the brilliance of lightning, mixed with earthly particles, is produced by the strike of a great whirlwind when opposing and favoring winds collide. 35.
The wind usually releases this during unseasonable rains; for during the monsoon season, these dust particles do not spread through the air. 36.
There are three types of phenomena: earthly, watery, and fiery. These arise from the burning in the depths of the earth, O King, within the crevices of the ground. 37.
Earthly dust is lifted up by the collection of fierce winds and brought into the Cloud Sphere <note>term: megha maṇḍala; the atmospheric layer where clouds form</note>, becoming free of impurity. 38.
Through the mixing with water droplets, a solidification <note>original: piṇḍī-bhāva; forming into a mass or ball</note> occurs here. These are seen falling, and then they dissolve again upon the earth. 39.
During the rainy season, the solar rays are honored <note>meaning: reflected or refracted</note> within the layers of the clouds. There, they produce many colors, mixed with the elements of fire, earth, and water. 40.
Those mixed with fire appear yellowish; those mixed with water appear blue; and those mixed with earth [appear in other hues].

<summary>This page describes the concentric islands of the Earth (Krauñca, Gomedaka, Puṣkara) and provides a physical explanation for meteorological events, attributing lightning and thunder to the interaction of the subterranean Vaḍavā fire, wind, and solar heat.</summary>
<keywords>cosmography, Vaḍavāgni, Krauñca island, meteorology, lightning, thunder, Cloud Sphere, monsoon, rainbows, Munīśvara</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from Munīśvara's geographical descriptions to his atmospheric physics. Having previously discussed the "Submarine Fire" (Vaḍavāgni) at the South Pole, he now uses that heat and the movement of the winds to explain light phenomena like rainbows, halos, and twilight.</meta>

The redness in the path of the sun's rays is caused by their interaction with the atmosphere (41). When these rays are gathered in the sun, it creates an appearance that the wise call a rainbow <note>term: Śakracāpa; literally "Indra's bow," the traditional name for a rainbow</note>. 42.

When the rays of both the sun and the moon are swirled by the wind into a circular form, they create various colors in the cloudless sky known as a halo <note>original: pariveṣa; often refers to a lunar or solar corona or halo caused by ice crystals or dust</note>. 43.

The stars <note>original: tārakāḥ</note> move through the heavens. Some of them stay very far beyond all the wandering planets, while those that are unstable and move rapidly <note>likely referring to meteors or "falling stars"</note> reside below the moon and are dependent upon it. 44.

Between the junctions of the cold and hot water-filled regions and the Great Wind <note>original: pravaha-māruta; in ancient Indian astronomy, this is the cosmic wind that keeps the planets in their orbits</note>, and when the eastern wind becomes still, these stars fall to the earth like sparks of light. 45.

Waterless clouds, thinned out by the wind and containing only a trace of vapor, rain down upon the surface of the earth. 46. The earth, heated by the sun's rays and dried by the wind, supports particles in the form of smoke. 47.

When these are dissolved by the heat of the sun, they become a collection of dust. This causes the appearance of clouds during the twilights of the day. 48. Dust and smoke rising from the earth spread to the horizon; when mixed with the sun’s faint rays, the sky appears reddish. 49. This thinning and scattering of the sun's light creates various colors that are seen, just like a rainbow. 50.

This twilight glow <note>original: sandhyā-rāga</note> should be understood to occur at the beginning of the day (dawn) and the end of the day (dusk). On the night of a full moon, the moon likewise creates this effect with its rays at the start of the night. 51.

Thus, the description of the nature of fire and the influence of the Submarine Fire <note>original: vāḍava</note> located in the ocean has been explained incidentally. 52.

***

**An Objection Regarding the Bridge of Rama**

Now, a question is raised: If the inhabited territory is strictly north of the ocean, how then could the Bridge <note>term: Setubandha; the "Bridge of Rama" or Adam's Bridge, a chain of limestone shoals between India and Sri Lanka</note>—so famous among the people and described in the history of the Raghu lineage—be possible or logical in this geographic model? 

To this, the following is said: The earth was dug out by the sons of King Sagara <note>A legendary king of the Solar Dynasty whose 60,000 sons dug the earth to find a lost sacrificial horse, thereby creating the ocean, which is called *Sāgara* in his honor</note> while they were searching for the horse released for the horse-sacrifice <note>term: Aśvamedha; a major Vedic ritual where a horse is set free to roam, and the land it traverses is claimed by the king</note>. It was through this digging that the water flowed around Lanka...

<summary>Munīśvara explains the physics of rainbows, halos, and twilight as interactions between solar/lunar rays and atmospheric dust or wind. He then addresses a geographical contradiction between scientific models and the mythological account of Rama's bridge to Lanka.</summary>
<keywords>rainbow (śakracāpa), halo (pariveṣa), atmospheric dust, falling stars, twilight (sandhyā), submarine fire (vaḍavāgni), King Sagara, Bridge of Rama (Setubandha), Lanka</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on atmospheric phenomena and moves into "Bhugola" or spherical geography. The author, likely Munīśvara, is now defining the specific geographical boundaries and latitudinal divisions of the earth's surface using the prime meridian of Lanka as a reference point.</meta>

<margin>Siddhānta Sārvabhauma</margin> ...is thirty-two <note>likely referring to a distance in Yojanas or degrees</note>, following the logic of what has been established. 54. Some say this region is situated in the middle of the Salt Ocean <note>original: kṣārābdhi</note>, and thus there is no error. However, this is not correct, because the movement to the other islands occurs to the north of the Salt Ocean. 55. If one were to accept that [faulty] premise, the earth would consist of only one island, because there would be no distinction between the islands and the oceans. But the earth is composed of Seven Islands <note>term: Sapta-dvīpa; the traditional Purāṇic cosmography of seven concentric island-continents</note>; therefore, the traditional view of the Sages <note>original: ārṣaṃ; "of the Ṛṣis"</note> is the one that is logically sound. 56.

From the center of Lanka <note>term: Laṅkā; in Indian astronomy, this represents the intersection of the equator and the prime meridian, not necessarily the modern island of Sri Lanka</note>, one should imagine lines of degrees surrounding the globe in every direction. Using Lanka as the center, one should draw circles at intervals of 9 degrees up to 50 degrees. 57. In the northern division, the line of the Salt Ocean and the boundary of the Snowy Mountains <note>original: Himādri; the Himalayas</note> form a circumference. The land in the middle is indeed the region of Bharata <note>original: Bhārata-varṣa</note>, which is famous among those who understand the globe of the earth. 58.

At a distance of 46 degrees and 25 minutes from the center of Lanka, there is a semi-circle; beyond that is the Golden Peak mountain <note>original: Hemakūṭa</note>. The beautiful land located in the interval between Lanka and this mountain is Kinnara-varsha. 59. At 7 and a half degrees from there <insert>measured from the previous boundary</insert>, there is a semi-circle which marks the Niṣadha mountain. The land in the middle, up to the Hemakūṭa mountain, is shaped like a bow-string; this region is called Harivarsha. 60.

In the same way, using the coordinates described from the city of Siddhapura <note>term: Siddhapura; the city located on the equator exactly opposite to Lanka on the other side of the globe</note>, there are three semi-circles representing the mountains named Śṛṅgavat, Śukla, and Nīla, occurring in that order. 61. Following this method, there are three regions <note>original: varṣas</note> belonging to these: Kuru-varsha, Hiraṇmaya-varsha, and Ramyaka-varsha, which are inhabited by people. 62.

The Mālyavān mountain and the city of Yamakoṭi <note>term: Yamakoṭi; the city 90 degrees east of Lanka on the equator</note> are situated at 64 degrees and 45 minutes. From the city of Romaka <note>term: Romaka; the city 90 degrees west of Lanka on the equator, often associated with the Roman West</note> lies the Sugandha mountain. Both the Nīla and Niṣadha mountains touch these ranges. 63. The land between Yamakoṭi and the Mālyavān mountain is called Bhadrāśva by the greatest of sages. The land area between the city of Romaka and the Sugandha mountain is known here as Ketumāla. 64.

The width of each of these mountains, starting with the Himalayas, is 2 degrees. Whatever has been stated here regarding these divisions is certainly and permanently true. 65. The Niṣadha, Nīla, and Sugandha mountains...

<summary>This page describes the mathematical geography of the Earth, defining the boundaries of various regions (Varshas) and mountain ranges based on their angular distance (degrees) from the prime meridian at Lanka.</summary>
<keywords>Bhugola, Bharatavarsha, Lanka, Hemakuta, Yamakoti, Romaka, coordinates, Siddhanta, geography</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical geography of the earth's surface to the mythical and cosmological description of the world's center. It details the region of Ilavrta and the central axis of the world, Mount Meru, following the traditional descriptions found in Puranic literature and astronomical treatises like the Siddhanta Sarvabhauma.</meta>

Located between the Malyavan mountains is the region of Ilavrta <unclear>covered and surrounded</unclear>, a place <term>crowded with the playgrounds of the immortals</term> <note>original: amarakehlikulāyasāmakulaṃ; referring to the gardens where the gods sport</term> situated upon a <term>beautiful golden surface</term> of the earth. 66

Here indeed is <term>Mount Meru</term> <note>the sacred five-peaked mountain of Hindu, Jain, and Buddhist cosmology that is considered to be the center of all the physical, metaphysical and spiritual universes</note> standing in the very center, <term>composed of gold and jewels, the abode of the gods</term>. Here, the <term>experts in the Puranas</term> <note>scholars of ancient Indian chronicles and lore</note> beautifully describe its origins and nature. 67

<term>Mandara</term>, <term>Gandhamadana</term>, <term>Vipula, and Suparshva</term>—<term>these are the supporting mountains of Meru</term>, located in the directions starting from the East. 68 

In sequence, there are the great trees <term>known as the Kadamba, Jambu, Pippala, and Vata</term> <note>the Rose-apple, Fig, and Banyan trees that mark the four quarters</note> which are situated on the northern landmass of the island; this is the true greatness of that land. 69

From the <term>crushing of the mountain-sized Rose-apple fruits</term> <note>Jambu-phala</note> flows the <term>Jambu River</term>, full of sweet juice. The mud of its banks turns into <term>Jambunada gold</term> <note>a legendary variety of gold said to be produced by the river’s sediment</note>; the hosts of gods and perfected beings <note>Siddhas</note> constantly drink from it, turning away even from the desire for celestial nectar. 70

The celestial forests of <term>Chaitraratha, Nandana</term>, and then <term>Savita-bhadra and Vaibhraja</term> are found there; these are the most beautiful groves found in succession in these regions. 71

There is the <term>lake known as Arunoda, and then the Great Lake</term> <note>Mahabhadra</note>, and the <term>lake of white waters</term> <note>Shitoda</note>. At these lakes, the wives of the gods and the celestial beauties, <note>Apsaras</note>, who are weary from play, take delight in their desire for water-sports. 72

Upon the summit of Meru is the <term>City of Indra</term> <note>Shakrapuri</note> in the East, known as <term>Amaravati</term>. In the direction of the Fire-god <note>Southeast</note> is <term>Tejovati</term>, the city of Agni. Then there is the city of Yama <note>the Lord of Death</note>, 73

which is <term>called Samyamini</term>. In the Southwest direction belonging to the guardian Nirriti is the <term>city of Krishnangana</term>. In the West is the city of Varuna <note>the Lord of Waters</note> called <term>Shraddhavati</term>. 74

In the Northwest is the <term>city named Gandhavati</term> belonging to Vayu <note>the Wind-god</note>. In the North is <term>Mahodaya</term>, the city <term>belonging to Kubera</term> <note>the God of Wealth</note>. In the Northeast direction belonging to Ishana <note>Shiva</note> is the city called <term>Yashovati</term>. 75

In the <term>middle of this Golden Mountain</term> dwell other gods and perfected beings. There, the sun <margin>perpetual =</margin> remains fixed at the <term>first point of Aries</term> <note>Meshadi; the vernal equinox</note> on the <term>ecliptic</term> <note>the sun's annual path</note>, which makes the day [constant] for the gods. 76

The mountain has <term>three beautiful golden peaks</term>. Thus <term>Mount Meru</term> is described with its moving slopes. On one of its high summits, there is a <term>golden region that touches the very sky</term>...

<summary>This page provides a vibrant geographical and mythological description of Mount Meru, the cosmic center of the world, detailing its golden landscape, celestial rivers, and the eight cities of the directional guardians (Dikpalas).</summary>
<keywords>Mount Meru, Ilavrta, Jambu River, Amaravati, Dikpala, celestial geography, Puranic cosmology, Jambunada gold</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological description of the world's center, moving from the geography of Mount Meru to the sacred descent of the River Ganges and the subsequent division of the land of Bharata (India) into nine regions.</meta>

<margin>
Siddhanta Sarvabhauma 8
5
</margin>

In the middle of the residence known as **Vaikuntha** <note>original: Vaikuṇṭha; the celestial abode of the Supreme God Vishnu</note>, the Lord who holds the discus, the conch, and the mace <note>traditional attributes of Vishnu</note>—whose prowess is celebrated—is eternally cherished by the Goddess of Fortune. 78-79

From the lotus feet of **Vishnu** <note>original: Viṣṇu-pada-kamala</note>, the **Ganges** <note>the holy river Ganga</note> flows down onto the summit of the **Golden Mountain** <note>Mount Meru</note>. From there, she moves in four directions, filling the lakes situated there. 79

The stream of the Ganga, joining with the red-tinted flow, is named **Sita**; it passes through the forest of the "Beautiful Horses" <note>original: bhadra-turaṅgamāraṇye; likely referring to the region of Bhadra-ashva</note> and the great lake known as **Chakra**. Another branch, called **Chaksus**, flows through the pure land of Ketumala. 80

The branch known as **Bhadra**, with its continuous flow of water, reaches the northern region of **Kuru**. That same stream from the foot of Vishnu which flows toward the land of **Bharata** <note>ancient India</note> is known by the name **Alakananda**. 81

Even a person who has committed the most strictly forbidden acts, or one who is feared by the gods themselves, is purified by the mere sight of this river. In this world, the Ganga playfully honors both **Vishnu** and **Shiva**. 82

In this land of **Bharata**, between the gate of the **Himalayas** <note>original: Himādri</note> and the ocean, the dimensions are calculated through measurements of the eastern and western parts. By degrees <note>likely astronomical coordinates</note> involving parts of thirty-two, and seven and a quarter, or eight parts, the boundary is marked. 83

By adding thirteen parts <note>original: atricandra; literally 'sages' (7) and 'moon' (1) interpreted as 13 or 17 depending on the system, but here likely numerical codes</note> and eighty-nine parts toward the ocean, the sign should be fixed. In the interval between these two points, one should mark the boundaries. 85

Toward the east, by the sequence of the movement of the shadows <note>a reference to calculating longitude/latitude using a gnomon</note>, with six and a half units, the expert should draw two signs and create a line between them. 86

Now I shall describe the divisions of the **Siddhanta-khanda** <note>the geographical sections of the astronomical treatise</note> and their names in their proper order as they have been spoken. 87

According to the sequence of the directions, starting from the East: **Aindrata**, **Tamraparna**, **Gabhastiman**, and the **Varuna** division. From there, the division of **Dharma** and **Bhagyaka**. 88

Then there is the northern **Meru-khanda** <note>the region of the central mountain</note>. In all of these, various peoples reside. In the very center lies **Kumarikakhanda**, where the system of social classes is established. 89

<summary>This page describes the divine descent of the River Ganges from Vishnu's feet to Mount Meru, where it splits into four branches to water the world, and outlines the geographical and mathematical divisions of the Indian subcontinent into nine traditional regions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the cosmological and geographic survey of the world, moving from the sacred geography of Bharata (India) to the structure of the subterranean realms and the relative time-keeping between the four cardinal cities of the ancient Indian globe.</meta>

The extent of the beautiful land of **Bharata** <note>original: Bhārata-khaṇḍa; ancient India</note>, beginning from **Lanka** <note>the city on the equator used as the prime meridian</note> and other points, is established in sequence. Beyond that, one should contemplate the arrangement of the land according to its measurements. 61

The seven principal mountain ranges here are known as the **Clan Mountains** <term>Kulachala; the seven foundational mountain chains of the subcontinent</term>: the **Mahendra**, the **Shuktiman**, the **Malaya**, the **Riksha**, the **Pariyatra**, the **Sahya**, and the **Vindhya**. 61-62

The earth is a sphere <note>original: gola-rūpā</note> containing hollows within its center. These are the regions of the **Netherworld** <term>Pātāla; the subterranean realms</term>, seven in number:
<margin>Level</margin>
**Atala**, **Vitala**, **Sutala**, **Talatala**, **Mahatala**, **Rasatala**, and **Patala**. These seven levels of the netherworld are illuminated by the light of the jewels on the hoods of the serpents, rather than by the sun. In these realms dwell the races of demons <note>original: asura-gaṇāḥ</note> and the **Nagas** <note>divine serpent-beings</note>, who sport there with divine and beautiful women, along with the **Siddhas** <note>perfected celestial beings</note>. These places shine with a luster like glowing gold. 62-63

Because the earth is held by the many hoods of the Lord of Serpents <note>original: Phaṇīndra; referring to Shesha or Ananta who supports the world</note> situated in the netherworld, it may occasionally tremble when his hoods move, yet even so, the earth remains stable for the sake of the people. 64

The realm of mortals <note>original: bhūrlokaḥ</note> exists on the surface of the globe in the region of **Jambu-dvipa** <note>the central continent</note>, at the center of which stands **Mount Meru**. Above this are the higher realms of the Sun and the celestial worlds, ending with the realms of **Tapah** <note>the world of penance</note> and **Satya** <note>the world of truth</note>. 65

When the sun rises in the city of **Lanka**, it is simultaneously midday in the city of **Yamakoti** <note>the cardinal city to the east</note>. At that same moment, it is sunset in **Siddhapura** <note>the city at the antipodes, 180 degrees away</note> and midnight in **Romaka** <note>the city to the west, often identified with the Roman West</note>. 66

Where the sun is seen to rise, that is the east; where it sets, that is the west. **Mount Meru** is famously situated in the north, in the middle of the waters. 67

If one travels one-fourth of the earth's circumference to the east, there lies **Yamakoti**. To the west lies **Romaka**. To the south, on the same line, lies **Lanka**, and to the north is **Mount Meru**. 68

By this same logic, everywhere in the world...

### The Chapter on Instruments

At this auspicious time <note>The text mentions a specific time and perhaps a location involving "Amera," likely referencing a patron or a place of composition</note>, I, the astronomer, for the great satisfaction of my students, shall now describe the various powers and functions of instruments. These shall be explained with great clarity, following the teachings of the illustrious **Bhāskara** and others. 1

#### The Circle Instrument
<term>Chakra-yantra; a graduated metal disk used for measuring angular distances</term>

One should have a circle made of copper or bronze. It should be marked with 360 degrees <note>original: khāṅgarāma; a numerical code where Kha=0, Aṅga=6, and Rāma=3, read right-to-left as 360</note>. In a chosen spot on this circumference, a supporting rim or "girdle" should be attached. 2

A pivot or pin <term>kīla</term>, with a length equal to half the diameter <note>the radius</note>, should be placed exactly in the center. Similarly, a base or "ground" for the instrument is conceived, held by a chain <note>original: śṛṅkhalā; used to suspend the instrument so it hangs vertically</note>. 3

At both ends, and at the central mark of its support, an eye-hole should be made. This is done so that the orb of the **Sun** may be observed through the rim. 4

Now, from the position of the hanging chain, the degrees that fall from the pivot represent the parts of the circle. In the middle of this "instrument-ground," the degrees observed are the **degrees of elevation** <term>unnatāṃśa; the height of a celestial body above the horizon</term> of the observed sphere. 5

The degrees resulting from the interval at the center are the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa; the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term>. From these, the division of directions is established at the center where the **azimuth circle** <term>dik-vṛtta; the horizontal circle of directions</term> meets. 6

<margin>The known side</margin>

The upright pivot made on the instrument, and the interval between the eastern line and the tip of the shadow... <unclear>five</unclear>... these measures in digits <term>aṅgula; a traditional unit of measure roughly the width of a finger</term> are derived from the diameter of the instrument. 7

Associated with the pivot of the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palaprabhā; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</term>...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "Circle Instrument" (Chakra-yantra) begun on the previous page. Having established the conceptual framework of the Cosmic Egg, the author Munīśvara now provides specific trigonometric formulae for determining the Sun's position and the time of day, followed by a transition into the physical observations of the seasons, specifically noting regional variations in Kashmir.</meta>

<margin>Calculated from the zenith distance =</margin> ...<unclear>the Sun</unclear> divided, the result diminished by the remainder and added to the <gloss>pillar of the gnomon</gloss>. From the string, the distance of the pin in the North-South direction is determined. 8

Multiplying that distance by the sine of altitude and dividing by the measure of the pin, the result is the corrected value. The solar amplitude <note>original: agrā; the angular distance of the Sun from the East or West point on the horizon</note> is then subtracted or added depending on whether the direction is North or South. 9

Multiply the solar amplitude by the hypotenuse of the equinoctial shadow <note>original: palakarṇa; a constant used in local latitude calculations</note> and divide by the radius to find the sine of declination <note>original: krāntijyakā</note>. Subtract the square of that from the square of the radius; the square root of the remainder is the "great sine." Multiply the sine of declination by its own shadow; 10

Divide by twelve and then by the radius. <margin>Sine of altitude × 12 × Radius / ... × 42 =</margin> This result, divided by the cosine of declination, gives the sine of the ascensional difference <note>original: carasiñjinī; used to calculate the length of the day</note>. From the radius and the sine of the altitude, the day-sine and the Sun's position are determined through division. 11

This gives the "desired time" <note>original: iṣṭakāla; the time elapsed since sunrise</note>. When subtracted from the midday time, the result is the time from the previous or following period. The arc of this value, multiplied by six <margin>Multiplied by 6, then divided by 1 ... / 3</margin> and divided, gives the hours and minutes elapsed since sunrise or until midday. 12

Multiply the sine of declination by the radius and divide by the sine of the maximum declination <note>original: jina-tattva-jyā; specifically 24 degrees in Indian astronomy</note>. The resulting arc gives the Sun’s position. In the first quadrant, it is the actual value; in the second, subtract it from the sign; in the third, add it to the sign; and in the fourth, subtract it from the full circle. Thus, the **True Sun** <note>original: sphuṭo bhāskaraḥ; the mathematically corrected position of the Sun</note> is revealed. 13

Through these mathematical degrees, the seasons—beginning with Aries and the spring—are established. However, scholars note that the manifestations of these seasons vary by country and region, as the world is diverse. 14

The appearance of fresh mango sprouts, the gathering of dark clouds, and the extreme clarity of water reflecting the moon—all these are the visible markers of the passing seasons. 15

Thus, for the benefit of the "Middle Land" <note>original: Madhyadeśa; the heartland of North India</note>, poets and astronomers have described the beauty of the seasons, starting with Spring, following the ancient wisdom of **Bhāskara**. 16

In the **Land of Kashmir**, <margin>specifically regarding...</margin> these signs appear differently. Because of the constant abundance of snow there, the signs of mango blossoms and <gloss>arriving</gloss> summer heat occur only rarely or at different intervals. 17

When the shadow falls upon the <term>Kīlaka</term> <note>the central pin or gnomon of the instrument</note> of the machine, and the planet's reflection is seen within the instrument, one must determine the North or South direction carefully. 18

One should then observe the planet's reflection between the two points... 2

# Si. Sā. Mau. <note>original: "Si Sā Mau," abbreviation for the Siddhānta Sārvabhauma by the 17th-century astronomer Munīśvara</note>

If the fixed pole <term>dhruva: the celestial pole or a fixed starting point for measurement</term> is separate from the planet, the planet should be added to or subtracted from it as appropriate. 19 Then the planet's position in the secondary region, the difference between the base and the interval multiplied by three and divided by the measure of the gnomon-pin, the result's arc is the "arrow" <note>śara: the sine of the celestial latitude or an angular offset</note> of the planet. 20

If there is a divergence in the planet's position, it should be placed in the designated spot. From the rotation of the celestial sphere, the segments are determined, as is the altitude of the planet. 21 Following the previously mentioned method, the true position of the planet is calculated sequentially. From the planet's light and the corrected previous observation, the ascendant <term>Lagna: the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment</term> and the shadow are determined. 22

During the day, if the direction is unknown to the observer, the time cannot be known. At night, one should use the position of the Sun increased by six signs <note>original: ṣaḍ-yuta-arka; adding 180 degrees to the Sun's longitude to find the point exactly opposite it in the sky, used for nighttime calculations</note> to determine the time elapsed since sunset. 23 This method is always valid. Thus, the time elapsed at night is understood through this process. This section is well-known and understood by all. 24

All the variations of the plate's placement described here are clearly explained in the *Yantra-cintāmaṇi* <note>The "Jewel of Instruments," a famous 15th-century treatise on astronomical instruments by Chakradhara</note>; therefore, I have not described them in detail here. 25 One should construct a board <note>phalaka</note> twenty-six finger-widths <term>angula: a traditional unit of measure, roughly 1.9 cm or 3/4 inch</term> in length and <margin>As the Sun, p. 22</margin> ten finger-widths in width. From the base of the instrument, markings such as lines and plumb-lines are made. 26

In this circular arrangement, a hole should be made in the center, described as having the width of one finger. A straight rod <note>śalākā</note> should be placed there, extending outwards. 27 A thick, straight strip <note>paṭṭī</note> is made with a sight at one end, two finger-widths in size. It should be attached loosely so that it can rotate freely. 28

The circumference of the circle is marked with 360 degrees. On this, the "Hypotenuse of the Latitude" <term>akṣakarṇa: the hypotenuse of the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox, used to determine local latitude</term> is marked. The radius <note>trijyā</note> multiplied by the Sun’s day-sine <note>dyujyā</note>... <unclear>calculation</unclear>... 29

This marking should be made by a calculator <note>gaṇaka: a mathematician or astronomer</note>. The sine of the ascensional difference <note>carajyākā: the difference between the actual rising of a sign and its rising on the equator</note> multiplied by the Sun's position and divided by the radius gives the result. This is used for calculations within the instrument. 30

In this way, the "Board Instrument" <note>Phalaka-yantra</note> is held. On its sides, the light of the Sun, equal to the ascendant, is observed. 31

### Siddhānta Sārvabhauma <note>original: "Si. Sā. Mau."; the "Universal Doctrine" written by Munīśvara in 1646</note>
### Verse 31-40

At the thirtieth division of the circumference, the wise observer should fix the position of the Sun <term>Taraṇi: the Sun; specifically referring to the solar longitude</term>. 31 Here, having placed the plate <term>paṭṭikā: a calibrated strip or plate used on astronomical instruments</term> at the stated solar degree, the mark that appears on the instrument board <term>phalaka: the main body or "face" of the instrument</term> indicates the stars. Below the north-pointing cord, the distance between the circumference and the vertical line determines the "inclination of the hour" <note>namra-nāḍyāḥ: the time-angle or the depression of the hour-line</note>. 32 

Having determined the sine of the co-latitude <term>lambajyā: the trigonometric sine of the angle complementary to the observer's latitude</term>, the sine of the time in <term>ghaṭikās: units of 24 minutes</term> should be calculated. This sine, when inverted, is placed on the ground-plane. If the shadow falls exactly on the mark of the gnomon-pin <term>yaṣṭi: a small rod or stylus used to cast a shadow</term> at the thirty-degree point, then that is the shadow's measure. 33 

The proof <term>upapatti: the mathematical derivation or rationale</term> for this should be done on a well-drawn circle. From that, the Sun-god described the four-sided instrument called the Board. 34 

Following the previously mentioned method, the width of the radius <term>trijyā: the "radius-sine" or the sine of 90 degrees</term> is twelve digits <note>original: arkāṅgula; 'Arka' (Sun) represents the number 12</note>. In a place calculated by the final sine <term>parāntyā: the sine of the maximum declination</term>, the length is measured as if at the North Pole <term>sumeru: the mythological mountain at the North Pole, used as a reference for 90-degree latitude</term>. 35 

This circle should be set up as explained by the ancient masters; it must be fashioned with care. In the middle of its length, a suspension chain <term>śṛṅkhalā: a chain or ring used to hang the instrument so it remains vertical</term> should be held as the support. 36 

This instrument is made of two sections from the base. Following the previously mentioned method, the maximum sine of altitude <term>drgjyakā: the sine of the zenith distance</term> should be applied here. 37 

A line measuring one-third of the radius of the instrument is drawn. Where the shadow of the rays of the Sun <term>Aryama: another name for the Sun</term> falls, at the end of that calculation, a hole <term>chidra</term> is to be made. 38 

The circle is marked by the radius from the center according to the digits of the gnomon. In this circle, the degrees are marked for the purpose of knowing the Sun's altitude. The number 65 is noted <note>original: svarasāśca; 'sva' (1) and 'rasa' (6), though the context suggests a specific graduation of 65 or 66 units</note>. 39 

To understand the time in <term>ghaṭī</term>, a plate equal to the measure of the final sine is fixed at its base. A pin with a hole is provided there, through which the altitude is clearly revealed. 40 

According to the teaching on the "Great Gnomon" and the base, the product of the square... <note>The text breaks off into mathematical formulas involving the sine of the day and the radius</note>

<gloss>
The mark is the product of the equinoctial shadow and the hypotenuse. At that place, the vertical sine is composed of two times twenty-four degrees. The hypotenuse of the gnomon is always twelve digits. What is the hypotenuse?
The product is always the measure of the radius. The maximum sine of the time-arc is at thirty-three degrees or more. When the planet is placed on the plate, the result of the board... therefore the measure given for this board is established.
</gloss>

<margin>
That is to say, the offset of the direction is equal to the gnomon's hypotenuse. There, the gnomon is twelve digits. By the measure of the three qualities, the plate is fixed on the board. The gnomon is 30, and the shadow moves to the north. This is the method.
</margin>

<margin>
Thus it is well-said.
</margin>

### Verse 41–44

The portion of the arc known as the "sky-number" <note>khisaṃ-jñā: likely a specific numerical parameter used in the calculation of the sine</note> should be calculated using the radius <term>trijyā: the sine of a 90-degree arc, used as the standard radius in Indian trigonometry</term>. From this, the sine of the solar degrees is determined. 41 

By utilizing the cord <term>rajju: the suspension string of the instrument</term>, the board instrument <term>phalaka-yantra</term>, and the latitude <term>akṣa</term>, one calculates the "power of the gaze" <term>dṛg-balaya: the vertical circle or the force of the observed altitude</term>. 42 At the moment of sunrise, the light originates at the center; as time passes, the shadow moves accordingly across the earth. Thus, the tip of the shadow always lies in the direction opposite to the Sun. 43 

The interval between the shadow's tip and the solar mark on the circle indicates the degrees of solar altitude above the horizon <term>kṣitijonnatāṃśāḥ</term>. Their sine is the gnomon's sine <term>śaṅku-jyā</term>, and the "final sine" <term>antyā: a technical term for the versed sine of the hour angle multiplied by the day-radius</term> is measured by the length of the gnomon-staff <term>yaṣṭi</term>. 44 

### Verse 45–48

If the gnomon-staff is twelve digits <term>dvādaśāṅgula: the standard measure for a gnomon in Indian astronomy, roughly 9 inches</term> in height, then what is the hypotenuse of the shadow? In the same way, the desired "final sine" is produced from the tip of the shadow. 45 

The straight line drawn from the degree-mark on the board to the length-line <term>dairghya-rekhā: the vertical axis on the instrument board</term> is established through calculation. Here, the expert should place the plate <term>paṭṭikā</term> according to the mathematical demonstration <term>upapatti</term>. 46 

From the base of the plate, one should measure a number of digits equal to the Sun's position; the point where this falls on the circle is the shadow-tip. From there, one should mark a sign up to the length-line; that distance is exactly the "final sine" <term>antyā</term>. 47 

At noon, the observed "final sine" is calculated. By subtracting the remaining portion, the versed sine <term>utkrama-jyā</term> is found. According to the celestial hemisphere <term>gola</term>, the "ascensional difference" <term>carajyā: the difference between the local and equatorial rising times</term> is added or subtracted to find the midday "final sine." 48 

### Verse 49–53

Because of the latitude, the midday sine and the ascensional difference are used in sequence. There, on a specific part of the line, the "midday final sine" is clearly seen. 49 Below this, the line measured by the desired "final sine" becomes the versed sine. Within that circle, the sine is produced; from its tip, the elapsed units of time <term>nāḍyāḥ: units of 24 minutes</term> are determined. 50 

Therefore, with ease, the "final sine" is marked on the line via the gnomon-point. Following the order of the celestial sphere as previously taught, the line situated at the end of the length-line becomes the "full sine" <term>pūrṇā-jīvā: the standard chord or sine</term>. 51 

In this way, the measurement of the width and length of the instrument is perfectly achieved through the power of the "final sine" <term>antyā</term> marked on the plate. 52 

Thus, the "Solar Board" <term>Phalakārka: another name for the Phalaka-yantra when used for solar observations</term> has been described by me in detail, following the teachings of Bhāskara <note>original: "Bhāskara-uktataḥ"; referring to Bhāskara II (1114–1185 CE), author of the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>. This instrument should be used to calculate the time elapsed in the night and other such matters. 53

### Verse 54–55

A circle with a radius <term>trijyā: the standard sine of 90 degrees used as a base unit</term> should be carefully drawn upon a level patch of ground. In the center of this circle, a staff <term>yaṣṭi</term> equal in length to the radius should be fixed, pointing toward the zenith <note>original: koṇ-mukha, literally "facing the corner," here implying the overhead point or vertical alignment</note>. This staff must be established with great precision. 54 

A plumb line dropped from the tip of this staff represents the gnomon <term>śaṅku</term>. From the center of its base, the sine of the altitude <term>dṛg-jyā</term> is laid out. From the position of the staff, the tip of the shadow is then produced. 55 

### Verse 56–57

Where the shadow touches the edge of the circle, an east-west line is drawn, appearing like an arrow <note>original: śara-vat, likely referring to the segment of a circle or the "sine" line</note>. The interval between the northern and southern portions provides the known measure of the staff. Through the center of the radius <term>nābhi-trijyā</term>, one calculates the dimensions. 56 

By dividing the solar value, the resulting figure is joined to the end of the shadow. This, when multiplied by the measures of the gnomon, determines the solar amplitude <term>arka-jāgrā: the distance of the Sun at sunrise from the east-west line</term> in either the north or south direction. 57 

### Verse 58–61

Regarding the rising and setting points, using the method previously described as the "five planets" <note>pañca-gra-prākāra: a technical method for determining planetary positions or coordinates</note>, the sine of the altitude is adjusted, and the northern direction is fixed. 58 

The east-west line is maintained there. One should mark the path on the circle like a hunter tracking an arrow. Then, from the center, another line representing the day-radius <term>dyujyā: the radius of the diurnal circle on which the Sun moves</term> is established, following the same threefold essence. 59–60 

Between the center and the tips of the shadow-staff, the measure of the cord is taken. That measure represents the full sine <term>sampūrṇa-guṇo: the chord of the arc</term>. In whatever region this is used, that same arc appears on the second circle. 61 

### Verse 61 (continued)–62

By the number of divisions, the time elapsed since sunrise <term>ahargata</term> is found when the Sun is in the eastern hemisphere; if the Sun is in the western hemisphere, the remaining time until sunset is found. This is calculated using the line of the rising and setting sun on the eastern circle. 61 

Using the end of the cord and a twelve-digit gnomon <note>original: dvādaśa-nara, the standard 12-finger-breadth gnomon</note>, the beautiful state of the shadow is determined. The clear mathematical proofs <term>vāsanā: the underlying logic or derivation of a formula</term> for these calculations have thus been stated by me. 62 

### Verse 63–64

This instrument was described by Gaṇeśa <note>Gaṇeśa Daivajña, author of several 16th-century astronomical treatises</note> for the purpose of understanding time. It should be constructed with its various parts according to one’s own requirements, ensuring it is neither too thick nor unstable, and derived from the circles and arcs. 63 

Beyond these measures, the instrument should be made in correct proportion, following the numerical specifications of its various angles. It must always be fashioned with a stable support to ensure its length and orientation remain true for the intended calculations. 64 

From the base and all around, the total length and dimensions...

The text appears to be following the methods of Gaṇeśa Daivajña’s sixteenth-century astronomical traditions.</meta>

### The Construction of the Sight-Holes

For the purpose of allowing light to enter the different divisions, holes should be fashioned in the instrument. In this manner, the external lines are directed toward the interior. 65 

A hole should be made exactly at the head in the center of the interior to serve as a support. Through this, the gnomon <term>śaṅku: a vertical pillar or rod used to cast a shadow for measurement</term> is to be passed. From this central hole to the outer edge, the length of the gnomon determines the divisions of the instrument as established in the scientific treatises. 66 

### Calculating Time and Shadow Lengths

The external gnomon should be measured in finger-breadths <term>aṅgula: a traditional unit of measurement approximately the width of a thumb</term>. Using the method previously described, a wise practitioner should determine the sine of the altitude <term>unnata-jyā: the sine of the Sun's height above the horizon</term> for the desired time of day <term>ghaṭī: a unit of 24 minutes</term>. 67 

The solar sine of the zenith distance <term>nata-jyā: the sine of the angle between the Sun and the point directly overhead</term> is divided by the sine of the altitude. This result provides the shadow length in fingers for the desired time. From the hole to the markings, the measure in fingers is laid out on a level surface. 68 

<margin>
4
</margin>

When the instrument is suspended by a chain, the gnomon is positioned so that its shadow falls upon the level surface. From the tip of the shadow to the designated mark, one should calculate the elapsed time in units of time <term>nāḍikā: synonymous with ghaṭī, a 24-minute period</term>. The remaining sections of the wall or instrument face are used to calculate the intervals between these time divisions through proportion. 69 

### The Mathematical Rationale

**The Proof:**
In this construction, the staff represents the vertical line and the ground represents the horizontal base. At the moment of sunrise, the shadow’s proportions are determined within the celestial sphere. From the sine of the altitude and the gnomon's height, the shadow length in fingers is derived according to the stated rule. 70 

### The Wheel Instrument (Cakra-Yantra)

Now, following the same logic, the construction of a circular instrument is described. A fine wheel-shaped instrument <term>cakra-yantra</term> should be crafted from metal or other suitable materials. Its radius should be equal to twelve fingers <note>original: vyāsārka-bhāgena; "arka" is a numerical code for 12, referring to the 12 suns of the zodiac</note>. 71 

At the center, a pin or gnomon equal to twelve fingers should be fixed, or it may be slightly smaller if needed for the specific scale. The circumference is then divided into segments. 72 

A wise person should draw individual lines from the base of the gnomon to each of these markings on the perimeter. On the surface of these divisions, the markings should be inscribed clearly so they may be read sequentially even by those who are not highly learned <note>original: strī-paṇḍitārha, literally "suitable for women and scholars," implying the instrument is so user-friendly that even those without specialized training can use it</note>. 73 

The sine of the zenith distance divided by the sine of the altitude, when adjusted for the specific hour, yields the shadow's measure in fingers. By subtracting the gnomon's height, the individual units for each unit of time are established. 74

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing and calibrating astronomical instruments. While the previous page focused on the board-style instrument and the shadow-casting gnomon, this section introduces more specialized sighting instruments and the methods for marking directional lines and time-units (nāḍikās) based on solar positioning.</meta>

### Stability and the Gnomon Shadow

The sighting instrument <note>original: "damsa-yantra," likely a variant of "drish-yantra" or "darsha-yantra," meaning an observation instrument</note> should be held or fixed such that it does not wobble. The shadow of the gnomon <term>śaṅku: a vertical rod used to cast a shadow</term> must fall upon its own designated plate. From the distance between the root of the gnomon and the end of the shadow, the knowledge of the time-units <term>nāḍikā: a unit of 24 minutes</term> is obtained. 75

### The Circular Instrument and Directions

Next, consider the instrument fashioned in the form of a half-circle <note>likely referring to a Dhanur-yantra or "Bow instrument"</note>. On its circumference, the markings for the Sun’s shadow are made. Facing the instrument, the front-to-back axis represents the East-West direction <term>pūrvāpara: the line from East to West</term>, while the side-to-side axis represents the North-South direction <term>uttara-dakṣiṇa: the meridian line</term>. 76

The solar amplitude <note>original: "agra," the distance of the Sun from the East-West line at sunrise/sunset</note> in degrees should be calculated for one's own direction. From the tip of the base <term>bhuja: the side of a right-angled triangle, often representing the sine of an angle in these contexts</term>, the rising and setting points are determined on the circumference. After establishing the path of the Sun, the hour angle <term>nata-nāḍī: the time-distance from the meridian</term> and the base must be calculated. 77

### Calibration and the Equinoctial Shadow

The measure of the desired gnomon is calculated here. The equinoctial shadow <term>palaprabhā: the length of the shadow cast by a 12-finger gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</term> is used with a standard gnomon of twelve fingers <note>original: "arka," a code word for 12, referring to the 12 names of the Sun</note>. The resulting values are added or subtracted according to the direction of the Sun's declination to find the position of the shadow’s tip. 78

This calculation involving the radius and the sine <term>jyā: a mathematical function similar to the modern sine</term> should be held using a marking rod <note>original: "śalākā"</note>. At the top, a thread <note>original: "sūtra"</note> marked with degrees is attached. This instrument is said to provide the desired gnomon and the base. 79

### Alignment and the Plumb-Line

From the point where the plumb-line <term>lamba: a weighted string used to ensure verticality</term> is attached to the instrument, a distance equal to the versed sine <term>śara: literally "arrow," mathematically the distance between the arc and its chord</term> is measured. In this manner, a mark is made on the instrument at the position of the specified base. 80

Once marked, the lines for the rising and setting points are drawn, following the numerical values of the base. These lines should touch the sine markings and extend toward the North-South markings; these are known as the "mother lines" or principal meridian lines of the instrument. 81

### Measuring the Time of Day

The instrument should be marked with the units of time elapsed since sunrise <term>unnata-nāḍikā: the "elevated" time units</term>. A wise practitioner should place the instrument on a level surface, correctly aligned with the cardinal directions, during the morning or afternoon parts of the day. 82

Depending on whether it is before or after noon, the pointer-rod <note>original: "śalākā"</note> should be adjusted using the sines and versed sines. The rod is turned to face the solar disk <term>sūryabimba: the visible circle of the Sun</term> so that its shadow falls exactly along the plumb-line of the horizon. 83

Where the shadow meets the midday line, one finds the time elapsed since sunrise or the time remaining until sunset. If the shadow falls between markings, a proportion <note>original: "anupāta"</note> must be calculated to find the exact time. 84

### Measuring Distances on Earth and Sky

Now, regarding a desired pole <note>original: "yaṣṭi"</note> placed on the ground or a riverbank: when an object is visible in the sky, its measurement is determined by...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical and astronomical instructions attributed to Bhaskara, specifically focusing on the use of the Staff Instrument (Yasti-yantra) for triangulation and the calculation of heights and distances (the 'Vamsha' or shadow-pole problems).</meta>

<margin>SSB 8</margin> <note>original: "si-sā-bho," abbreviation for Siddhānta Śiromaṇi Bhāṣya, page 8</note> This staff instrument, which has been described, is the singular essence of all observation. 85

By looking at a planet or object through one <gloss>eye</gloss> from both the tip and the base of the staff, and by drawing a line from the base of that staff, the distance on the ground is the base <term>bhuja: the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term>. 86

The perpendicular distance from the eye to the ground is the vertical <term>koṭi: the upright side of a right-angled triangle</term>. The staff itself is the hypotenuse <term>śruti: the diagonal connecting the base and the vertical</term> produced by the observation. From this direct perception, all measurements are known by wise men through the application of mathematical proportions. 87

The base and the vertical, when multiplied by the radius and divided by the staff length, yield the sines of the zenith distance <term>nata-aṃśa: the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term> and the altitude <term>unnata-bhāga: the angular height above the horizon</term> respectively. This is the method for determining those values. 88

<margin>9</margin> By observing the Pole Star <note>original: "dhruva"</note> <gloss><unclear>...and</unclear></gloss> the Sun, the result of the base calculation provides the equinoctial shadow <gloss>equinoctial shadow</gloss> <note>original: "palabhā," the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</note>. One should establish this using the stated method when the opportunity arises. 89

Regarding the measurement of a bamboo pole <note>original: "vaṃśa," often used in Sanskrit geometry as a placeholder for any vertical height to be measured</note> and similar objects: the product of the base and the height, divided by the vertical, gives the distance from the root of the pole to the observer on the ground. 90

The ground distance multiplied by the vertical obtained from sighting the top of the pole, and divided by the sighting distance <note>eye-level</note>, gives the result: the height of the pole. 91

By merely sighting the tip, one can find the interval between the ground and the base of the pole. The ground measurement is found by using the sine <gloss><unclear>...sine</unclear></gloss> corresponding to the pole’s tip. 92

Whether sitting or standing, the locations of the base and the vertical must be known; here, the vertical is known from the sighting of the top of the pole. 93

The distance from the eye is determined by the vertical divided by its own base; from this, the ground and the pole’s height are known separately as before. The proof for this lies in the known <margin>94</margin> distance from the root of the straight pole.

By the previously described method of using a "hidden" assumption <note>original: "gupta-prakalpa," likely a method of algebraic substitution or the "rule of supposition"</note>, the nature of the degrees is found through two separate sightings. 95

The vertical is multiplied by its own... here, the height of the pole is found by adding the height of the observer’s eye to the result of the sighting. If the pole's measurements are equal, there is no difference in the pole's dimensions. 96

By subtracting the unknown <note>original: "avyakta," an algebraic term for a variable</note> from the other side, the distance on the ground is established. This objective for the purpose of sighting was previously taught by Bhaskara in his own treatise. 97

For example: Sighting the top of a pole situated above a desired staff, the base is seen to be four cubits; the staff is then moved <note>or "placed"</note> such that it is some number of fingers... 98

<margin>85</margin>

This vertical distance, measured in digits, serves as the vertical side <term>koṭi: the upright side of a right-angled triangle</term> for both observations. If the distance between the two poles is known, then the expert observer can determine the heights of the poles and their shadows. 99

By observing the reflection of the tip of the object in water, one can also perform these calculations as previously described. However, in this case, the line of sight <note>original: "śrautraṃ"</note> connects the eye and the reflection of the tip, even when the observer is not directly at the site of the object. 100 etc.

In the descriptions of instruments provided by previous teachers, this particular apparatus was indicated. For the benefit of those who construct instruments <note>original: "yantrakāri"</note>, it has been described here in a concise manner. 101

The "self-moving" <note>original: "svayaṃ-vaha," referring to automated or perpetual motion devices</note> variety of instruments mentioned by the ancients has also been described by me here. Because the logical proofs derived from the celestial sphere <note>original: "gola-yukti"</note> were not explicitly provided, there might be doubts regarding their accuracy; therefore, this explanation serves as a refuge for the student. 102

Thus, in the *Siddhāntatattvārtha* <note>meaning: "The Essence of the Truth of Astronomical Treatises"</note> composed by Munīśvara, within this **Siddhānta-sārvabhauma** <note>meaning: "The Universal Emperor of Siddhāntas"</note>, concludes the description of the Extensive Instrument. 1

***

I shall now state questions designed to provide four-fold answers, intended to sharpen the minds of young students who have mastered the previous texts. These questions are presented in a new style. In the calculation of the cumulative growth of time, the positions of all the planets, beginning with the Sun, are revealed through the calculation of the center of the solar disc. 2

The remainder of the intercalary month <term>adhimāsa-śeṣa: the fraction of a 13th lunar month remaining in a given period</term>, the positions of the Sun and Moon, and the remainder of the omitted lunar days <term>avama-śeṣa: the fractional part of "skipped" days in the lunar calendar</term> are determined from the residues. 3

He who, by his own intellect, can determine the true position of the Sun and Mercury <note>original: "budha"</note>... if these are joined in a specific calculation, it is praised by the learned. 3 <note>Note: verse numbering repeats or is inconsistent in the original manuscript.</note>

For the purpose of finding the desired weekday, the total number of elapsed days <term>ahargaṇa: the count of days since the start of the current cosmic epoch</term> is required. One must understand that the remainder of the intercalary month, when added to or subtracted from the total intercalary months of the Great Cycle <term>yuga: a vast period of millions of years</term>, is consistent with the proofs established by the ancient masters. 4

From the remainder of the terrestrial days <note>original: "akṣa-dhi-śeṣataṃ"</note>, one should subtract the values related to the Sun and the twelve signs. By joining these with the total intercalary months of the Great Cycle, the wise student understands the true motion of the celestial bodies. 5

Even if a true intercalary month has occurred but was not mathematically obtained <note>original: "alabdhaḥ," suggesting a discrepancy between observation and calculation</note>, one should calculate the remainder of the intercalary month using the cycle of weekdays. By adding or subtracting the values of the Great Cycle, the remainder is known by those who understand the movements of the Sun. 6

<summary>This page contains complex mathematical formulas for calculating astronomical constants such as elapsed intercalary months (adhimāsa) and the residual of omitted days (avama-śeṣa). These calculations are vital for determining the Ahargaṇa, or total day-count, which serves as the basis for all planetary position calculations in Hindu astronomy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions for determining the Ahargaṇa (total elapsed days). The previous page concluded with calculations involving fractions of omitted days and solar variables. Here, Munīśvara introduces the Kuttaka (Pulverizer) method, a classic Indian algebraic technique for solving indeterminate equations, specifically applied to synchronize lunar and solar cycles.</meta>

Combined with 3, and divided, the result stated by the Sun and the Moon <note>original: "Puṣpavanta," a poetic term for the two luminaries</note> is added to the total day-count. 18. Here is the logic: a wise person should contemplate the application of the Rule of Three <term>trairāśika: a mathematical proportion used to find an unknown fourth value from three knowns</term> using the Constant Pulverizer <term>sthira-kuttaka: a fixed algorithm used to solve linear indeterminate equations of the form ax + c = by</term>. The intercalary months <term>adhimāsa: extra lunar months added to align with the solar year</term> of a world-age are multiplied by the elapsed time and decreased by the intercalary residue. 19. The solar months of the world-age are the divisors. From there, the multiplier and the quotient are found through the Pulverizer. The elapsed intercalary months are the result of the subtraction, while the lunar days are the measure for the additional months. 20. The dividend <term>bhājya: the number to be multiplied (a)</term>, the divisor <term>hāra: the dividing number (b)</term>, and the additive <term>kṣepaka: the constant added or subtracted (c)</term> should first be reduced by a common factor if possible, to simplify the Pulverizer process. Both the dividend and divisor must be divided by the same number. 21. When two numbers are mutually divided, the last non-zero remainder is their common factor <note>this describes the Euclidean algorithm for finding the Greatest Common Divisor</note>. The dividend and divisor, once divided by this factor, are called "firm" or "reduced" <term>dṛḍha: coprime numbers</term>. 22. Mutually divide these two reduced numbers (the dividend and divisor) until the remainder in the dividend becomes one. Place the resulting quotients one below the other in a column, and place the additive below them. 23. Multiply the second-to-last number by the one above it and add the number below it. Repeat this "churning" process from bottom to top until only two quantities remain. The top value, when divided by the dividend and reduced, gives the quotient; the other, divided by the divisor, gives the multiplier. 24. Thus, the process is completed. If the number of quotients in the chain is odd, the resulting multiplier and quotient must be subtracted from their respective reduced divisors and dividends to find the true values <insert>for the Pulverizer multiplier</insert>. 25. For a wise person, the multiplier and quotient are thus fully determined. In a "summation" type equation <note>original: "yogaje," where the additive is positive</note>, these are the results; in a "subtraction" type <note>original: "viyogaje," where the additive is negative</note>, they are applied accordingly. 26. Calculating each remainder individually would be tedious. Therefore, one should assume "one" (unity) as the residue to determine the base multiplier and quotient, which are then multiplied by the actual reduced additive. 27. Through these various methods, the multiplier is found. When the residues are multiplied by three and divided, the results represent the elapsed intercalary months from the beginning of the Great Age, which are then added to the lunar count. 28. The total day-count is then derived. Following the method of the predecessors, the remaining lunar days are determined. These two values <note>Sun and Moon</note> should be calculated according to the methods taught by Bhāskara <note>referring to Bhāskara II, the famous 12th-century mathematician</note>. Thus, a wise man should understand this. 29.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical demonstration of the "Constant Pulverizer" method used to calculate astronomical periods. It follows the previous discussion on reducing large numbers to their simplest terms (Greatest Common Divisors) to solve indeterminate equations related to the lunar and solar calendars.</meta>

<margin>Siddhānta Sārvabhauma, Folio 11</margin> Now, for the purpose of generating the specific values using the **Constant Pulverizer** <term>sthira-kuṭṭaka: a mathematical algorithm used to solve linear indeterminate equations to find integer solutions</term>, the dividend, divisor, and remainder are set down.

The **Dividend** <term>bhājya: the number to be multiplied by the unknown</term> is 1,563,336. The remainder is <unclear>0</unclear> <unclear>and 1</unclear>. 

Following the rule of "mutual division of the two" and so on, when the remainder is zero, the **Common Factor** <term>apavartaka: the greatest common divisor used to simplify the equation</term> is found to be 24. The **Divisor** <term>hāra: the number that divides the product</term> is 1,555,200,000. 

By dividing both by this common factor (24), the **Reduced Dividend** <term>dṛḍha-bhājya</term> becomes 661,258 <note>Mathematical note: 1,563,336 divided by 24 is actually 65,139; the manuscript or transcription may contain a calculation error here</note> and the **Reduced Divisor** <term>dṛḍha-hāra</term> becomes 64,800,000.

The **Reduced Additive** <term>dṛḍha-kṣepa</term> is the residue of the intercalary months <term>adhimāsa-śeṣa</term> divided by the same factor. Now, to simplify the Constant Pulverizer, the value of 1 is used as the additive. By mutually dividing the dividend and divisor, the **chain of quotients** <term>valli: a column of integers derived from the Euclidean algorithm</term> is produced: 

10, 6, 1, 5, 13

The divisor is 64,800,000. /4/2/2/2/ Below this, the additive is placed. Through the process of multiplying the term above by the one below and adding the last, and then discarding the last term repeatedly, the **pair of results** <term>rāśi-yugma</term> is produced: 

189,900 and 672,581,360.

These are then reduced by the dividend and divisor respectively. To achieve the desired result, these are multiplied by the simplified remainder. This is how the total **Omitted Days** <term>avamāni: the difference between lunar and solar days</term> for the world-age are calculated, as explained previously.

The Reduced Dividend and Reduced Divisor are 30... from the Moon, this is obtained. Following the previously stated method, the Reduced Divisor is naturally produced... solar... at the end of the solar month... below the solar degrees. 31. 

The value 1,030,000 <note>original: "kharāmattahaṃ," a code for the number 1,030,000 using the Katapayadi system</note> is the divisor for the omitted days. Through this, the minutes and other units are found. These are multiplied by the Sun's position... the Moon is moved by the strength of the lunar days. 32. Therefore, by the result known as "Omitted" mentioned before, combined with the midnight position, the remainder is obtained. This is the understanding of the ocean-like knowledge of the calculation of time. 33. 

For the Constant Pulverizer, the **Dividend** 25,082,252 and the **Divisor** 2,603,000,008 are reduced by four.
