# The Calculation of Planetary Positions and Day-Counts

Having observed the "New Sprout" <note>original: "navāṅkure"; likely referring to the *Navāṅkura* commentary or a specific sub-section of the text</note> of the teachings from the feet of the venerable paternal uncle and astrologer, the wise man should consider the seat of all knowledge regarding the **Pulverizer** <term>kuhaka: another name for the *kuṭṭaka* or "pulverizer" algorithm used to solve indeterminate equations</term>. **[Verse 34]**

By taking the signs, degrees, minutes, and seconds <note>original: "rāśi-bhāga-kalā-vikalābhir"</note> and multiplying them by the given measures of the heavenly bodies, one obtains the result. Learn the sequence for bringing forth the **day-count** <term>dinagaṇa: also known as Ahargaṇa, the number of elapsed days since the start of an epoch</term> in the matter of the Pulverizer. **[Verse 35]**

In the progression of the **Yuga-cycle of the Sun** <term>yugamaṇḍala-sūrya: the total revolutions of the sun in a cosmic age</term> and others, the signs and following units are reduced to seconds. These are then multiplied by the **Reduced Earth-days** <term>dṛḍha-pṛthvī-dina: the simplified total of terrestrial days in a Yuga cycle</term> and divided. **[Verse 36]**

By the numbers 11,266... <note>original: "kha-kha-ṣaṣṭi-nava-dvi-bhūmibhiḥ"; coded numbers representing 0, 0, 60, 9, 2, 1</note>, the result is obtained. When the remainder is subtracted, and divided by the "sky-sky-sixty-nine-twelve" <note>original: "kha-kha-ṣaṣṭi-navārka"; referring to the divisor for seconds in a circle, 2,160,000</note>, one should add one to the result of the division. **[Verse 37]**

The remainder of those parts becomes the "divisor" <term>hara</term>, which is 11,266... The first remainder, acting as the "multiplier," is increased by the divisor and multiplied by the "reduced earth" value. **[Verse 38]**

If this produces the remainder of the seconds, then the calculation is well-performed. If it is the opposite, then the **Yuga-count** <term>yugaṇāt: the accumulation of days</term> is truly obtained together with it. This is the truth. **[Verse 39]**

The **Reduced Revolutions** <term>dṛḍha-bhagaṇa: the number of planetary orbits simplified by a common factor</term> should be the multiplier. When these are divided by the "Pulverizer-divisor" <term>kuhaka</term>, they should become pure (yielding no remainder). These day-counts, joined with the reduced divisors, are used by the wise for various applications. **[Verse 40]**

The number for purification is seven-limbs-element-direction... 16,561,977 <note>original: "saptāṅga-tattva-śudigaṃ pañca-nṛpati"</note>. The solar cycle value is four-eight-four-four-zero-nine-eight-four-five-seven: 4,844,098,457. From this, the day-counts of the remainder are produced automatically. One should continue the process until the other planets, following the astronomical rules of the **Moon and Shastra** <note>original: "śaśā-śāstrīty-ādito"</note>, are resolved. **[Verse 41]**

As shown here, the **Constant Pulverizer** <term>sthira-kuhaka</term> is to be performed. The process results in the following:

| **Divisor** <term>hāra</term> | 3,548,778,457 |

In this pair of heaps, 453,616,561,977 is the desired day-count. Divided by the gods, it gives the light of the days. The day-counts of the **Omitted Lunar Days** <term>avama: "intercalary" negative days to sync lunar and solar calendars</term> are added to the accumulation of lunar days. This is separated from the calculation of the **Intercalary Months** <term>adhimāsa: the extra months added to the lunar calendar to match the solar year</term>. **[Verse 42]**

<margin>12</margin> Subtracting those from the value above, and then from the remainders multiplied by thirty <note>original: "khāgni" (sky=0, fire=3), representing 30</note>, the elapsed **lunar days** <term>tithi: a lunar day, 1/30th of a lunar month</term> are found as the result. **[Verse 43]** When divided by twelve <note>original: "sūryaiḥ", using the 12 Suns as a numerical symbol</term>, the result—excluding the solar transition—should be known as the months elapsed here, starting from **Chaitra** <note>the first month of the Hindu lunar calendar</note>. This direct calculation is easy; the inverse process <note>original: "viloma"</note> should be understood here by the wise. **[Verse 44]**

One who desires to know the precise, subtle time near the beginning of **retrogression** <term>vakra: the apparent backward motion of a planet as seen from Earth</term>, through this well-joined method... **[Verse 45]** ...these matters regarding the knowledge of precise time are inherited from the Teachers; the rest is like a flash of insight. <margin>12</margin> To derive the very precise **Mean planet** <term>madhyakhaga: the position of a planet if it moved at a constant speed in a perfect circle</term> from the **True planet** <term>sphuṭagraha: the actual observed position of a planet</term> by mathematical operations, this inverse process is firmly established. I bow to the greatest among those who know the **Shiromani** <note>referring to Bhaskara II’s famous 12th-century treatise, the *Siddhānta Shiromani*</note>. **[Verse 47]**

In the opinion of those for whom the Sun and Moon become slow through the **Equation of Center** <term>mandaphala: the correction required to account for a planet's elliptical orbit</term>, the Mean positions are obtained by applying that stated correction inversely just once. **[Verse 48]**

<margin>Toward</margin> In the opinion of those where the Sun and Moon are already True, the person should apply the stated correction derived from the "slow-motion" <note>original: "mandottha"</note> inversely to refine them. **[Verse 49]** By this, those two True positions are refined; in the inverse calculation, they become the Mean positions. This is my opinion, which is joined with logic and the definitions of the **Siddhanta** <term>Siddhānta: traditional Indian astronomical systems</term> such as the *Surya Siddhanta*. **[Verse 50]**

For Mars and the other planets, from their True positions, the correction called "slow" <note>manda</note> is determined by the rules. Because that is to be calculated from the Mean position (which is unknown), one must repeatedly apply the inverse of the **Equation of Anomaly** <term>śīghraphala: the correction for the Earth's own motion</term> to the True positions to arrive at the unchanging Mean. Through these inverse steps, the "sons of the Earth" <note>Mars and others</note> become Mean planets. **[Verse 51]** From the True planet, the "slow" correction is applied inversely, then the "fast" <note>shighra</note> correction is applied inversely; repeatedly applying the "fast" correction results in the Mean position. **[Verse 52]** Some say the Mean is derived by applying both the "slow" and "fast" corrections inversely; it is by these that the great result of the "fast" correction is first given...

Therefore, the experts have stated that from these True positions, one must calculate repeatedly <note>This refers to an iterative mathematical process to reach a precise value</note>. The reason for this is that at the end of the **slow-motion correction** <term>mandaphala: the equation of center</term>, the True positions are derived from the well-known apparent motions. **[Verse 55]** In the case of True planets, the correction for slow motion is applied inversely to the True position, and then the **fast-motion correction** <term>śīghraphala: the equation of the conjunction, accounting for the Earth's own movement</term> is applied. There, the slow-motion result is found in the middle, and from that, the so-called "fast" True position is derived inversely. **[Verse 56]**

In this way, the Mean of the **Sun** <note>original: "himadyoḥ", literally "cold-rayed," usually the Moon, but contextually referring to the solar/lunar light-givers</note> is found repeatedly. # Regarding the Nature of the Shadow: The product of the Equinoctial Shadow and the Radius divided by the Sine of one and a half signs
<note>The "sine of one and a half signs" refers to the sine of 45 degrees, which is a constant in these trigonometric derivations.</note>

<margin>13</margin> The **square of the shadow** <term>bhā-varga</term> is derived from the square of the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā: the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</term>. From this calculation, the **hypotenuse of the shadow** <term>bhā-karṇa</term> is established in the form of a square root. **[Verse 3]** The product of the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā: the standard radius of the trigonometric circle</term> and the equinoctial shadow, related to the desired **sine of latitude** <term>akṣajyā</term>, is divided by the square roots of those values.

Radius · Pa · 1 <gloss>Radius · Pa / Bha · A · 1</gloss> <note>These glosses appear to be shorthand for mathematical ratios: Radius (Trijya), Palabha (Pa), and likely Azimuth or Amplitude (A/Bha)</note> 

The **amplitude of the Sun** <term>arkāgrā: the distance of the rising sun from the east point</term> multiplied by the **hypotenuse of the latitude** <term>akṣa-karṇa</term> and divided by the local latitude yields the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term>... 22. From these, the sine of declination and the **corner declination** <term>koṇa-krāntijyā: the declination specifically calculated for the intermediate directions/corners</term> are established. 

Radius · Pa · A · 22 <gloss>Radius · Pa / Bha · A · 22</gloss> 

Where the product of the radius and... <unclear>pk 5</unclear> is divided by the root, thus the **cosine of latitude** <term>lambajyā</term> is found. 

Radius · Pa / Pa · A · 1 5 <gloss>Radius / Bha · A · 1 5</gloss> 

The square of these parts should not be used in vain for the realization of the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku: the vertical pillar used to measure shadows</term> and the **Sine of the Jewel** <note>This may refer to a specific prized trigonometric result or a corrupted term for a specific sine</note>. In this way, the cosine of latitude, multiplied by the **cosine of the corner declination** <term>koṭijyā</term> (which consists of its own day-radius), and divided by the radius, gives the result arising from the latitude. Because it is in the northern direction, it is established as **North** <term>saumya</term>. 

The corner declination, multiplied by the cosine of the latitude (in the form of the desired sine of latitude), and divided by the radius, yields the result arising from the declination. By using both the **azimuth sine** <term>digjyā</term> and the shadow, one should find the desired difference. In the lineage of great teachers, I now explain the realization of the famous **Siddhanta** <term>system of astronomy</term> composed by the venerable **Bhaskaracharya** <note>Bhaskara II (1114–1185 CE), the author of the Siddhanta Shiromani</note>. **[Verse 68]**

From the difference of the squares of the **azimuth sine** and the **amplitude** <term>agrā</term>, the number 244 <note>The text uses the word-numeral "Tigma-veda-ashvi-chandra," which traditionally would be Chandra (1), Ashvi (2), Veda (4), Tigma/Sun (12), but the scribe has noted 244 here</note> first arises. Then, the product of the amplitude and the hypotenuse of the shadow is given another name. The azimuth sine is divided... 66. With the radius, the sum of the squares of the movement of the equinoctial shadow is the divisor <term>hara</term>. **[Verse 69]**

...from the first value joined with the square of the last, then joined or subtracted with the last... 10. **[Verse 70]** In the Northern or Southern hemispheres, when the product of the desired time occurs, and the azimuth sine is less than the amplitude, then from the square of the first, joined or subtracted by the second, the desired direction is found externally. **[Verse 71]**

The unknown result <term>avyakta-phala</term>, having made the corner and the amplitude as the sides, is found by the proportion of the sines. Thus, through the power of the azimuth sine, from the equality of the squares of the previous sides, the algebraic clearing <term>sama-śodhana: the process of simplifying an equation</term> is performed. **[Verse 72]**

From the algebraic method <term>bījataḥ: using algebraic analysis or "seed" mathematics</term> of extracting the middle value, the manifest value is again determined through equations. When the two bases are multiplied by each other's divisors, in the order of the same or different directions, the sum or difference divided by the sum of the hypotenuses reveals the equinoctial shadow in different hemispheres. **[Verse 76]**

When the two bases are equal, the method mentioned above does not apply to the problem; in that case, it falls outside the scope of the rule. **[Verse 77]** <margin>Or the visible ones.</margin>

Then, the value to be taken is proportional. **[Verse 78]** In this matter, by making the equality of terms through many forms, the wise can easily understand the method described in the **Science of Algebra** <term>Bījokta-vidhi: the rules of algebraic calculation</term>. 

This is the explanation: From the first base, the form of the unknown circular amplitude has three variations. From the second base also, there are three variations. From this proportion, in the circle of the Radius, the square of the Radius and so on are derived.

### Siddhanta Sarvabhauma 95
**Rama 1**

From the principles of the desired time and the elevation in *nadis* <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>, and having understood the sine of declination <note>original: "krantijya"</note>, one finds the value in the center to be 8. The square of the desired shadow is added to the square of the gnomon; the square root of this sum is the hypotenuse. 9 By this, the result is obtained: the square of the <term>Trijya</term> <note>the radius of the calculation circle, usually 3438 minutes of arc</note> multiplied by the equinoctial hypotenuse and divided by the sine of the elevation in *nadis*. 10 From this, the sine of the day-radius is produced. When the square of this is subtracted from the square of the radius, the square root of the remainder is the "reversed sine." This process yields the desired result for the southern hemisphere.

11 Regarding that elevation-sine, by the previously described method and using the sine of the day-radius, the time-sine is found. In this way, the time-sine is repeatedly refined, a process that is easily understood by those who focus on the calculations. 12 From the mathematical operations arising from the three signs <note>90 degrees</note>, the difference between them should be considered the arc of the elevation. If the elevation is less than that, the calculation proceeds; if it is greater, it cannot be done in that manner. 13 Having taken the sine of the elevation and the sine of the desired time, their sum is used to calculate the elevation-sine. By the power of the day-sine, the time-sine is determined as before, becoming increasingly accurate. 14 Now, through the refinement of the parts, the square of the base-sine and the sum of the multipliers are used to determine the middle path. This is the "seed of calculation" <note>the algebraic method</note> known to the experts. 15 The maximum time-sine is combined with the sum of the <term>Trijya</term>. Multiplied by the sum of the two squares, the maximum time-sine is the product of the sums. This is then multiplied by the sum of the radius. 16 When the square of the time-sine is established, it is combined with the square of the other multiplier and the square of the sum. From the total square, the other parts are removed. 17 Or, from the root, the first time-sine is subtracted. I shall now explain the reasoning behind this calculation using the value of the <term>Avyakta</term> <note>the "Unknown" or algebraic variable, like 'x'</note> divided by the radius and the sine of declination.

<gloss>Method</gloss> <term>Unknown</term> sine-base... <term>Unknown</term> sum 2. <gloss>Method 2</gloss> The sine-base arising from the declination is here the time-sine, combined with the sum. That sum is the square of the <term>Unknown</term> multiplied by the sum, resulting in the first <term>Unknown</term>-square. 18 By adding the maximum time-sine and the sum, the <term>Unknown</term> product is found. 19 In the second part of the step, the square of the product of the maximum time-sine and the sum is the third part. Below these is the divisor. 20 The square of the maximum time-sine itself is the square... <note>The text here uses algebraic abbreviations: Un-Sq, Tri-Sq, etc.</note> from the square of the <term>Unknown</term> time-sine, the root is taken from the square of the <term>Trijya</term> to find the common denominator. 21 Thus, <gloss>Method for the Unknown</gloss>, in the section of the <term>Unknown</term>, when the simplification is performed, the square of the <term>Unknown</term> is multiplied by the square of the sum of the maximum time-sine. This is the first part of the absolute form. 22 Two multiplied by the <term>Unknown</term> is the second multiplier, with the negative value placed thereafter.

### Calculation of the Sun on the Prime Vertical

One should calculate the hour angle in minutes <term>natakāla-nāḍī</term> <note>time measured in 24-minute units from the meridian</note> for the sun when it is positioned on the Prime Vertical <term>samamaṇḍala</term> <note>the great circle passing through the east and west points and the zenith</note>. By knowing this, the astronomer can determine the placement of the sun. 34
<margin>Siddhānta Śiromaṇi 16</margin>
The shadow of men <note>the gnomon shadow</note> occurs in various directions according to the deviation of the sun's path. 35
The sine of the declination <note>original: apama-jīvā</note> is derived from the square root of the difference between the squares of the hour angle and the latitude's shadow. By multiplying this by the radius and dividing, the sine of the hour angle is obtained through the equinoctial shadow <term>palabhā</term>. 36
Knowing the hour angle of a planet when it has reached the Prime Vertical, and because its nature is consistent, the declination of that planet is then determined. 37
<margin>16</margin>
By using the difference of the squares of the sine of the planet's position and the radius <term>tribhajyā</term>, and dividing by the square of the equinoctial shadow, the result is the square of the total sine. 38
From this, the sine of the declination is found by taking the square root. This calculation is described here so that it may be observed by those with a clear and focused intellect. 39

### Derivation of the Gnomon and Day-Radius

The calculation of the small parts of the degree is determined by subtracting from the sun's position. Here, the square of the sine of the hour angle is subtracted from the square of the radius, and the square root is taken. 40
<gloss>obtained</gloss>
By this sine, the radius <gloss>Radius</gloss> is used to determine the exact vertical gnomon <term>śaṅku</term> <note>a vertical pillar used to measure time and latitude by its shadow</note> and the "base" of the shadow. From these parts, the sine of the declination is firmly established. 41
<insert>Day-radius, Radius, etc.</insert>
Now, if the day-radius <term>dyujyā</term> <note>the radius of the sun's daily circular path</note> is unknown, how can it be found? It is found by squaring the terms. The square of the day-radius is found by taking the square of the radius and subtracting the square of the sine of the hour angle. 42
<gloss>Radius-squared: 144</gloss>
By dividing by the square of the radius, the proportions are established. Therefore, the day-radius is the divisor for the previous calculation. 43
Just as the square of the day-radius was unknown, the square of the radius is used as the dividend. Through proportion <term>anupāta</term>, the divisor is then multiplied by the square of the radius. 44
<gloss>Day-radius-squared: 1</gloss>
<gloss>Radius: 1</gloss>
When the divisor for the three signs <note>90 degrees</note> is processed, it becomes equal to the square of the day-radius, but the square of the declination <term>krāntijyā</term> remains as the remainder. 45
The divisor is established by the loss of the square of the day-radius in the previous step. The square of the declination, when divided by this, yields the square of the day-radius. 46
Therefore, the divisor plus one becomes the final divisor. From this, the sine of the declination and the radius are easily obtained. 47

### Determining the Sine of Ascensional Difference

The calculation of the sum of the sine of declination <term>krāntijyakā</term> <note>the sine of the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator</note> is presented here; the astronomers call this the "calculation of the imagined value." 48
Multiply the sine of the ascensional difference <term>carajyakā</term> <note>the difference between the right ascension and the oblique ascension, used to determine day length</note> by the radius <term>trimaurvyā</term> <note>the standard radius used in calculations, usually 3438 units</note> and divide by the square root of the difference between the square of the radius and the square of the equinoctial shadow. Then, by taking the root and multiplying by the sine of ascensional difference, the multiplier for the sun is obtained. 49
Here is the logical derivation: The sine of the amplitude divided by the radius results in the sine of the earth <term>kujyā</term>. By the twelve-digit gnomon and <gloss>radius squared minus sine of ascensional difference squared 1</gloss> the equinoctial shadow <term>palabhā</term> <note>the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</note> is known as the sine of the latitude. 50
The sine of the declination multiplied by the difference between the equinoctial shadow and the radius, then multiplied by the sine of ascensional difference, produces the sine of the latitude. This is described in the *Siddhānta* <note>the established astronomical doctrine</note> to prevent fear of error or confusion. 51

### Mathematical Proofs of the Divisor

This divisor's form is created through the square <insert>(radius squared - equinoctial shadow squared 1)</insert> of the segment of the radius. In all places, the square of the sine of declination is obtained as the pure result. 52
In the square of the sine of the amplitude, where the multiplier is <insert>radius squared minus sine of ascensional difference squared 1</insert>, the square of the equinoctial shadow is well-known. Because the square of the declination and the square of the planet's position cancel each other out through their subtraction, they vanish. 53
When the multiplier and divisor are equal to one-third of the equinoctial shadow, then the square of the result multiplied by <note>radius squared + equinoctial shadow squared 1</note> results in the divisor's form being established by the logic of the calculation. 54

### Challenges to the Astronomer

I consider him to be a Lord of Sages <term>Munīśvara</term> <note>likely a reference to the author himself, Munīśvara, or a title for a master astronomer</note> at this moment, who—knowing the sum of the sine of colatitude <term>laṅkajyakā</term> and the equinoctial shadow <term>palabhā</term>—can calculate the latitude shadow if he truly understands the refined calculation of such a sum. 55
Subtract the square of the sum from twice the square of the radius. The square root of this difference is then subtracted from or added to the sum, respectively. When this is multiplied by the first value of the sun, 55 [cont.]
And divided by the other, the result is the equinoctial shadow <term>akṣabhā</term>. By projecting the addition and subtraction again as a new sum, the equinoctial shadow is refined through the previously mentioned method. 56
The other value is multiplied by 12 and divided by the first. From this result, which is greater than the sun, the sum is refined. The equinoctial shadow is thus calculated in two ways through this process of descent. 57
I consider him to be a Lord of Sages <term>Munīśvara</term> at this moment, who—knowing the sum of the sine of colatitude and the hypotenuse of the shadow <term>dakṣa-śravaṇa</term>—knows how to calculate the latitude shadow <term>akṣahā</term> from that refined sum. 58
Subtract the square of the sum from twice the square of the radius. The square root of this is added to or subtracted from the sum. Multiplying the radius by the maximum declination 24 <note>original: paramāpabhāsa 24</note>, the result is the latitude shadow. 59
By taking that difference as the new sum and applying the previously stated method, the equinoctial shadow and sine of declination are refined as previously described. 60

### The Sun on the Prime Vertical

When the Sun <term>Mārtaṇḍa</term> enters the Prime Vertical <term>sammamaṇḍala</term> <note>the circle passing through the East, West, and Zenith</note>, and its shadow enters the four-digit mark in the sky, or when the time elapsed since sunrise is equal to the time until noon, do you know the declination of the Sun then? If so, I consider you a master of the night. <unclear>g</unclear> 61

### Calculations for the Prime Vertical and Earth-Sine

The sum of the squares here, divided by 61, provides the <term>iṣṭahṛti</term> <note>the "desired divisor," a technical astronomical constant used to find the sun's position</note> for the time elapsed from sunrise. This is to be projected onto the tip of the shadow. By multiplying the equinoctial shadow by the radius and dividing by the hypotenuse, one obtains the value for the <term>kujyā</term> <note>the "earth-sine," the distance between the rising-setting line and the diurnal circle's intersection with the horizon</note>. 61–62

Using the <term>cara</term> <note>ascensional difference</note> and other elements, the desired divisor should be calculated repeatedly. When the resulting value is purified, it becomes the clear hypotenuse of the shadow at the moment the sun enters the <term>samamaṇḍala</term> <note>the Prime Vertical; the great circle passing exactly East, West, and through the Zenith</note>. 63

### A Challenge to the Learned Astronomer

When the sun enters the Prime Vertical, its shadow is seen to be a certain number of digits <note>the standard unit of gnomon measurement is 12 digits</note>. If the month, the time in <term>ghaṭīs</term> <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>, and the lunar day are known, can you tell me the sun's declination? If you can explain this calculation clearly, then I shall consider you truly proficient in the great expanse of this science. 64

For this, the sine of the time elapsed from sunrise must be calculated as before, and that should be designated as the <term>iṣṭahṛti</term>. Then, by multiplying by the shadow of the sun and dividing by the <term>samashaṅku</term> <note>the gnomon's shadow when the sun is in the Prime Vertical</note>, the result relates to the <term>palabhā</term> <note>the equinoctial noon shadow</note>. 65

The <term>samashaṅku</term> multiplied by the equinoctial shadow and divided by the <term>akṣakarṇa</term> <note>the hypotenuse of the equinoctial shadow</note> results from the <term>antyā</term> <note>the sine of the total diurnal arc from sunrise to noon</note>. From the <term>cara</term> and other factors, the desired divisor and the sine of declination are obtained once more. 66

Knowing the <term>kujyā</term> and its derivation, a wise man can find the shadow and the sine of declination through his own intelligence. Among those who know the numbers and the celestial bodies, I consider him most excellent who can provide the essence of this calculation. 67

### Mathematical Ratios and Geometric Proofs

The <term>kujyā</term> is multiplied by 184; the result is then divided by the equinoctial shadow. Alternatively, the <term>kujyā</term> multiplied by the radius and divided by the <term>palabhā</term> becomes the <term>krāntijyā</term> <note>the sine of declination</note>. This is the logic explained here. 68

Regarding the altitude and the sine of the angle, the difference between the sine of declination and other factors is analyzed. What is the value when the sun's shadow represents the base of the triangle? The shadow previously described takes the form of the <term>koṭi</term> <note>the vertical side or "climax" of a right-angled triangle</note>. 69

In cases where there is a combination of the <term>koṭi</term> and the <term>bhuja</term> <note>the horizontal base of the triangle</note>, or a combination of the base and the <term>karṇa</term> <note>the hypotenuse</note>, one must calculate their individual values. O learned one, tell me these values quickly! 70

This method of calculation is understood by through the two-fold combination of three. Multiplied by the sum of its own kind, it produces the result of the desired object. 71

### Praise for the Great Astronomer Bhāskara

The mathematician who can derive the base, altitude, and hypotenuse from their sums and differences—that man is a true <term>Jyotirvit</term> <note>one who knows the lights; an astronomer</note>. He is like a lion standing proudly upon the forehead of an elephant <note>a traditional metaphor for a scholar who has conquered difficult problems or rivals</note>; such a man is <term>Bhāskara</term> and those who follow his path. 72

Through the multiplication of the desired quantity and by the power of knowledge, the result for the student is achieved. The previous examples serve as the demonstration for this calculation. 73

# The Earth-Sine

<warning>Yellow staining and slight smudging in the central column of the text, particularly affecting lines 3, 5, and 12.</warning>

->**The Earth-Sine**<-

Om. The <term>krāntijyā</term> <note>sine of declination</note>, the <term>sama-śaṅku</term> <note>the gnomon or its shadow when the sun is on the Prime Vertical</note>, and the <term>taddhṛti</term> <note>a specific trigonometric segment of the diurnal circle</note> reduced by the <term>kujyā</term> <note>earth-sine</note>, are measured together as twenty and sixty-six parts <note>represented by the words 'svarasa' (6) and 'vimshati' (20)</note>. Another set is measured as one hundred ninety-eight <note>represented by 'vasu' (8), 'anka' (9), and 'chandra' (1)</note>. Knowing the <term>agrajyā</term> <note>sine of amplitude</note> and the equinoctial shadow, I bow to Bhāskara <note>referring both to the Sun and the great 12th-century mathematician Bhāskara II</note>, who is the means of awakening the lotus-blossom of the astronomers. 74

The sine of declination, the gnomon on the Prime Vertical, and the value of the shadow of that <term>dhṛti</term> reduced by the earth-sine <gloss>i</gloss> <insert><unclear>??</unclear></insert>... having taken the sine of amplitude and divided it separately by those measures, the result should be the equinoctial shadow. From the hypotenuse of the equinoctial shadow and the calculation of the sun’s position, the divisor is found. By dividing the first sine and multiplying by sixty, the result is obtained. To that Bhāskara... 75

The sine of declination, the gnomon on the Prime Vertical, and the sum of the <term>taddhṛti</term> and the earth-sine together reach the measure of one hundred <note>encoded as 'pūrṇa-vadhya-vidhama'</note>. Another set equals three hundred eighteen <note>encoded as 'bhra-astra-bhu'</note>. Knowing these together with the sine of amplitude and the gnomon, I bow to the supreme Bhāskara, the method for awakening the lotus of the astronomers. 76

The sum of the sine of declination, the gnomon on the Prime Vertical, and the <term>taddhṛti</term>, when multiplied by the sun, becomes the shadow of the latitude. From that, the equinoctial shadow... if the declination is taken with the shadow of the sun and divided, then multiplied by twelve, it becomes the sine of declination... when there is equality between the two shadows, knowing their sum, and by the speed of calculation... by sixty... if you are a master of this field. 78

When there is a sum, whatever the material substance may be, one should separate it. From that, one should calculate the other materials according to the rules of the method. 79

These substances, multiplied by their own sums... seventy... separately... in the order of the sun, for those whose desired sum is given. 80

The sum of the sines, starting with the sine of amplitude and the sine of declination, is stated by the teachers to be two thousand and fifty, divided into different categories. If your mind is skilled in calculation, explain this! 81

Let the sphere be as it is—the famous branch of mathematics known as the <term>gaṇita-skandha</term> is well-established. It is achieved through the efforts of those who know the foundation of the latitude. Here is the discussion of the <term>vī-kathā</term> <note>perhaps a mathematical argument or discourse</note> regarding the positions of the declination and the sphere... those which are clear divisions of the Prime Vertical. Let them be projected as far as the sun's measure from the East point. 82-83

Having made this division, for each of these signs of the zodiac, in whichever division it commences, they say the measure follows in sequence. 84 91... risen...

# The Determination of the Houses

Other scholars have spoken of the <term>Bhavas</term> <note>the twelve astrological houses</note>, but those who truly understand the <term>Gola</term> <note>the celestial sphere</note> do not always agree with them. It is said that if there is a difference in the rising of the signs, it is due to the variation in shadows; therefore, nothing should be assumed without calculation. **85** Because the result obtained from the shadow of the Sun is not the same as the forward-calculated value, many have described this differently in their own treatises. For this reason, I shall explain the houses according to the actual position of the sphere, having determined it through my own intellect. **86**

The <term>Mṛtyu</term> <note>8th house</note> and <term>Dharma</term> <note>9th house</note> are calculated from the <term>Dvādaśa</term> <note>12th house</note>. This is a profound question regarding the difference between what is observable and what is calculated. From that, one should find the junction of the houses through the sum of the <term>Lagna</term> <note>the Ascendant</note> and the <term>Śaṅku</term> <note>the gnomon or sine of altitude</note>. **87** The square of the <term>Trijyā</term> <note>the radius of the circle, typically 3438 minutes</note> is used to find the result of the northern latitude of the houses. The <term>Jyā</term> <note>sine</note> which is produced by the proximity or distance of the house is considered the multiplier for the sines of the signs. **88** By the divisor and the individual degrees of the Sun, the arc is found; then, the previous house is added to the next. Thus, the three houses are stated, and by adding six signs to them, the remaining six are known. **89**

### The Logic of the Houses

Now for the proof: The position of the houses is established on the horizon. One should lead the circle to the observation point. Because of the sine of the latitude and the difference in the <term>Krānti-vṛtta</term> <note>the ecliptic or the sun's path</note>, the square of the <term>Trijyā</term> reduced by the square of the rough house-position becomes the square of the <term>Dṛgjyā</term> <note>sine of zenith distance</note>. From that, the square root of the base is found. **91** Where the circle of the rough house touches the ecliptic, that distance is measured by the sine of the degrees of the interval. **92** If the sine of the interval of the tenth house is taken by the divisor, then the sine of the houses is multiplied by the difference of the interval of the final sign... <note>The text here describes a complex proportional calculation to find the true cusp of the house</note> **93** The result obtained is the arc. This represents the very clear position of the house situated in the middle of the circle. When the tenth house is added to that measurement, it is correct. **94**

This is the true measurement of the <term>Sphuṭa-bhava</term> <note>the "clear" or mathematically corrected astrological house</note>. Adding six signs to these gives the opposite houses. This is the essence of the "Consideration of the Sphere." There is a further specific method for calculating the <term>Sandhi</term> <note>the junction or "cusp" where one house ends and the next begins</note>. **95** The degrees of the ecliptic are equal to the degrees of the <term>Lagna</term>. These are measured by groups of six. The "face" of these begins from Aries; the number of house-halves are calculated for the sake of determining the results of the planets. **96**

### Calculation of Geographic Distance

The learned astronomer, having known the science, should calculate the <term>Palāṃśas</term> <note>degrees of latitude</note> from the distance in <term>Yojanas</term> <note>a unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</note> between two places. The wise man should contemplate this repeatedly. **97** Multiply the <term>Yojanas</term> by 360 and divide by the <term>Kuvṛtta</term> <note>the circumference of the earth</note> in <term>Yojanas</term>. The result is the degrees of the earth's surface for one's own location. **98** Using the <term>Digjyā</term> <note>sine of amplitude</note> and the <term>Palabhā</term> <note>the equinoctial midday shadow</note>, and multiplying the radius by 12, the sines of the latitude and co-latitude are found. The difference in the southern direction is produced by the degrees of the distance. **99** When the sum of the earth's circumference is greater, the calculation involves the Sun... <gloss>note: the math here relates to the adjustment of the observer's latitude</gloss> thus the degrees of the body of the earth are known. **100**

**The Logic:** One should imagine the globe of the earth and then the celestial sphere...

# The Geometry of the Zenith and the Gnomon

At the <term>Zenith</term> <note>original: *Lamba-svastika*; the point directly above the observer</note> of one’s own city, and from there to any desired point, the truth of the distance in <term>Yojanas</term> <note>a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly 8-13 kilometers</note> is established. Because of its existence on the <term>Vertical Circle</term> <note>original: *Drig-vṛtta*</note>, the <term>Sine of Altitude</term> <note>original: *Drigjyā*</note> and the <term>Cosine of Altitude</term> <note>original: *Kotijyā*</note> are formed. For the desired eastern circumference, in the middle of the horizon, the directions are explained. **1**

With the <term>Radius</term> <note>original: *Trijyā*</note> as the hypotenuse, the sine of the direction is the base, and the sine of altitude is the hypotenuse for the cosine. Here, one obtains the result of the <term>Twelve Houses</term> <note>original: *Dvādaśa*</note>. From there, up to the <term>Base of the Gnomon</term> <note>original: *Śanku-mūla*</note>, the southern and northern boundaries are fixed. **2-13**

Touching the circle of that city, one should increase the calculation from the rising and setting points everywhere. From that, the base is known, and its lower surface is also divided into degrees. **3** On the southern side, the distance is called the <term>Gnomon’s Projection</term> <note>original: *Śanku-tala*</note>; it is the difference between the two sides. On the northern side, it is the sum. In calculations of many <term>Yojanas</term>, the gnomon's projection is inverted, and from the village's position, the <term>Sine of Declination</term> <note>original: *Apamajyā*</note> is produced. **4**

From these, the degrees of the arc are told. Up to the limit of the desired country, in the lower surface, it takes the form of the <term>Degrees of Latitude</term> <note>original: *Palabhāga*</note> of that desired place. **5** Here, the pole and the arrows of the stars, in the same quadrant or at their setting, are understood by the wise to happen simultaneously. Here, the measure of latitude is the boundary. **6**

For two stars that rise at the same time, the planet that is pure is the lord, and the other is different. From that, the <term>True Declination</term> <note>original: *Sphuṭa-krānti*</note> multiplied by the <term>Ascensional Difference</term> <note>original: *Charajyā*</note> is the sine. In a place where the latitude is equal to the true declination, **7** by that rule, at the end of the celestial sphere, the first and last are joined. At that time, the location is devoid of the <term>Sine</term> <note>original: *Kramajyā*</note>. **8**

The square of the <term>Radius</term> <note>original: *Trijyā-varga*</note> multiplied by the sines of the two positions is divided by the sine of altitude. The result is the square of the calculated <term>True Cosine of Declination</term> <note>original: *Sphuṭa-dyujyā*</note>. This is used where the rising and setting occur at the same time. **9-10**

If the difference between the degrees of the signs is more than a portion, then in that case, the sine and the <term>Versed Sine</term> <note>original: *Utkramajyā*</note> are used. **211** The derivation for the first planet is the sine of its zenith distance. From the center of the sky, the difference in the degrees of the signs on the celestial circle is determined by those parts. **212**

By the rule of the shadow, the desired gnomon is calculated. If the sine of the direction is divided by the sine of the latitude, **213** then by that rule, the sine of the day is found. The degrees of the pole are north of the two points where the vertical circle meets the horizon. **214**

In the other half of the celestial sphere, when the planet sets, the <term>Sine of Zenith Distance</term> <note>original: *Natatjyā*</note> from the nadir should be taken. From this, the opposite is true for the constellations at night in reverse order. **215** This is the calculation known by the experts of the <term>Sphere</term> <note>original: *Gola*</note>. Having abandoned the pride of one's own intellect, one should respect the rules given by the ancient teachers.

# The Observation of Chitra and Swati

Furthermore, an illustration regarding the distant stars is presented here: **216** In a country where <term>Chitra</term> <note>the star Spica</note> is perfectly visible on the eastern horizon and <term>Swati</term> <note>the star Arcturus</note> is rising or setting in the west, what are the <term>Latitude degrees</term> <note>original: *akshāmsha*; the angular distance north or south of the equator</note> that should satisfy the experts in spherical astronomy? **217** By obtaining the answer to this, one attains a sense of accomplishment. I shall now explain the method as previously conceived by the great sage <term>Munīśvara</term>. **218**

Take the <term>True Sine of Declination</term> <note>original: *sphuta-krānti-jīvā*; the sine of the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator</note> of Swati and multiply it by the <term>Radius</term> <note>original: *trijyā*; the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, typically 3438 arcminutes</note>. When this is divided by the declination of Chitra, the result is the sine of the amplitude. Thus, the clear declination of the Swati-point is established in the sky. **219**

The degrees located between the rising positions of Chitra and Swati are calculated. Those degrees which are measured from the east are determined by the count starting from Chitra, as the calculation must be performed using the rising points of Swati and others. **220** Using the <term>Cosine of the True Declination</term> <note>original: *sphuta-krānti-koti-jyakā*</note> of Chitra and the radius, the <term>Sine of the Direction</term> <note>original: *dig-jyā*</note> is known. From the result of the cosine, the <term>Versine</term> <note>original: *utkramajyā*</note> is derived through the radius. **221**

The result obtained from multiplying the <term>True Sine of Declination</term> of Chitra by the sine of the amplitude, when converted into degrees of arc, indicates those lands where Chitra and Swati appear on the horizon at the same time. **222** This should be deeply contemplated by all those learned in the science of the celestial sphere. From the logic of the principles stated, for those who reflect, the desired direction and the <term>Sine of Latitude</term> <note>original: *palajyā*</note> are determined. **223**

According to the teachings of the <term>Siddhānta</term> <note>the established mathematical treatises of Indian astronomy</note>, if the <term>True Sine of Declination</term> is equal to the sine of the latitude, then what would be the <term>Sine of the Direction</term> <term>Maurvi</term> <note>another term for the sine chord</note> for Swati? It would be equal to the radius. **224** This is proved by the proportion stated for Chitra. Therefore, it is not proper to say that Swati is situated on the horizon merely because of the calculation of the degrees of distance between the two stars. **225**

Without the <term>Latitude degrees</term> <note>original: *palāshān*</note>, the intersection of the eastern and western circles is not possible. In the intersection of the circles called the horizon, the primary degrees are said to be established. **226** This is appropriate when the sun is situated on the <term>Equator</term> <note>original: *vishuvan*</note>, as stated in the <term>Sphuta</term> <note>the corrected or "true" astronomical calculations</note> teachings. Therefore, anything else is considered disconnected from the reality of the sphere’s position and the mathematical path. **227**

If the <term>Sine of Altitude</term> <note>original: *drīgjyā*</note> of the planet at the meridian is written as the sine of the direction by some grace, then all the results regarding the fingers of the gnomon’s shadow would be rendered fruitless. **228** Even though there might be confusion of the intellect due to contradictions with the great sages such as <term>Brahma, Arka, and Chandra</term> <note>referring to the traditional authors of the Paitamaha, Surya, and Soma Siddhāntas</note>, this example stands as proof.

# Calculations for Mars and the Star Chitra

For the sake of the circle of scholars and brothers, and following the teachings of the learned <term>Prabharukara</term> <note>a scholar likely cited as an authority on light or planetary rays</note>: **228** When the celestial circle of the ancient sages is applied to <term>Mars</term> <note>original: *Kuja*</note>, the calculation should be made relative to its local position. If the sine of the desired direction and the sine of the amplitude are known, then the <term>Earth-Sine</term> <note>original: *Kujya*; a technical term in Indian astronomy representing the distance between the rising-setting line and the east-west line</note> is subtracted from the arrows of the sines... **230**

The sine of the true declination of <term>Chitra</term> <note>the star Spica</note> is multiplied by the corresponding factors. If the radius is the standard for Mars at any given time, then through the <term>Rule of Three</term> <note>original: *trairāshika*; a mathematical proportion used to find an unknown fourth value from three known values</note>, the degrees of its minutes are well-established. **231** If the distance between the two is less than the calculated degrees, then any discrepancy in the reading is due to an error in transcription. If one speaks of a greater value there, it is a mistake. **232**

The cosine of the true declination of Chitra is multiplied by the sine of the altitude... this is the famous method. Therefore, your statement is not incorrect; rather, in this hymn of calculation, the previous digit relied upon was different. **233**

## Determining Direction and Distance

From a known latitude, falling toward a desired country whose location is known by its <term>Yojanas</term> <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 15 kilometers depending on the system</note>: what is the direction of that desired place? Speak! What would be the degrees and minutes? **234** Multiply the sine of the degrees of the desired place by the hypotenuse; this shall be the northern direction. The shadow is stated in *yojanas*, and from the cosine, one finds the southern foot of the earth. **235**

When the latitude is less, it is north; if they are in different directions, they are added. By multiplying by the sine and dividing by the result, the distance in *yojanas* is brought to the eye. **236** Those degrees of the distance are equal to the multiplier. The reasoning here is easily understood by following the inverse method, as seen in various examples for the sake of easy comprehension. **237**

# The Royal Problem of King Shah Jahan

A certain master of sages, having studied the feet of the sun-like teachers <note>referring to the lineage of the astronomer Bhaskara</note>, resided in the capital city. This capital was filled with the light of the great jewels in the crowns of the heads of kings who bowed there. I speak now of the one named **Shah Jahan**. **238**

Having reached the Lord <note>likely referring to the Emperor or a high authority</note> at the city of Agra, and having taken his own glory from there, he now desires to go to **Kashi** <note>Varanasi</note>. Due to the confusion of his own haste, he does not know the path of the directions. At a distance of three hundred and fifty *yojanas*, in which direction is Varanasi located? Tell me correctly, O friend, and let your fame be celebrated among the virtuous. **239**

It is in this city <note>Kashi</note> that the Enemy of the Demon Andhaka <note>the God Shiva</note>, riding upon his bull, personally approaches at the time of death and whispers into the ear the "Crossing Word" <term>Taraka Brahma</term> <note>a sacred mantra believed to grant liberation at the moment of death in Kashi</note>. **240**

The King **Shah Jahan**, through the power of his feet which have struck the heads of the lords of the directions, has conquered the gathering of his enemies... <warning>Digital watermark across the bottom of the page obscures some text in the final line.</warning> **241**

In this context, the meaning of the "five-fold" path is also explained. Regarding the name "Shah Jahan" mentioned previously, if there is any doubt...

<summary>This page covers technical sine calculations for Mars and the star Chitra before presenting a geographical word problem involving the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan traveling from Agra to Varanasi.</summary>
<keywords>Mars, Chitra, Spica, Declination, Kujya, Rule of Three, Shah Jahan, Agra, Varanasi, Kashi, Munishvara, Siddhanta Sarvabhauma, Taraka Brahma</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from technical astronomical proofs to a historical-astrological eulogy of the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan. The author, Muniśvara, is using the capture of Balkh (1646–1647 CE) as a case study to demonstrate how astronomical calculations and "Yavana" (Persian/Islamic) calendrical systems can be integrated into the Sanskrit Siddhāntic tradition.</meta>

<margin>
Siddhānta Sārvabhauma Commentary
20
</margin>

Regarding the one who performed that action: the performer is the one who belongs to the <term>Hijri era</term> <note>original: *Hajjā ha*; likely a Sanskritization of the Arabic/Persian *Hijra*</note>. Known as the destroyer of many enemy kings, he is the most excellent **Shah Jahan**.

He is the "Excellent" (*Pa*) because he is the greatest and most distinguished, being the **Universal Sovereign** <note>original: *Sārvabhauma*; this is a pun on the title of the treatise itself</note>. This King, named Shah Jahan, by his own strength and heroism, seized the powerful land known as **Balkh** <note>a major city and province in modern-day Afghanistan; its conquest was a significant but costly military campaign for the Mughals in 1646</note> from his enemies. In the famous context of the <term>Yavana</term> <note>originally meaning Ionian/Greek, but by this period used to refer to Persian, Arabic, or Islamic cultures</note> methods, this event is tied to a specific date. 

The Universal Sovereign, intending to conquer that region, caused the land to tremble with the shadow of his advancing armies. Here, according to the secret codes mentioned in the astronomical sciences, the date is calculated following the "Yavana path." The <term>Era</term> <note>original: *Śaka* or *Sane* (from the Persian *San*, meaning year)</note> of this event corresponds to the current year. To calculate it: the "Senses" are **five**, the "Intellect and Organs" are **five**, and the "Pranas" are **ten**. Moving from right to left <note>a standard practice in Indian number-notation where the units come first</note>, this reveals the year **1055** <note>this refers to the Hijri year 1055 (approx. 1645–1646 CE), the start of the Balkh campaign</note> in which the seizure of Balkh was manifested. 

In this calculation, the sun's position and the signs of the zodiac are used as markers. The twelve months are well known; among them, the fourth month, known as **Tir** <note>original: *Tira-māsa*; the fourth month of the Persian solar calendar</note>, was the specific time of the event. Now, one might ask: why mention the capture of the Balkh country in a general work of science? It is done under the guise of describing a specific historical "Date" <note>original: *Tārikh*; the author uses the Persian term for history/date</note> to show the excellence of this mathematical system. The "Danda" <note>pun: both a measuring rod and military punishment</note> of the King was applied there. The "smoke" of the burning enemy branches rose into the sky, meaning the death of the rivals. Thus, the anxiety of the task was removed. 

Through this action, he is known as the "Universal Sovereign" (*Sārvabhauma*). It is certain that the task of seizing Balkh was truly miraculous. By using the phrase "The maker of the earth in the sky," the poet indicates the exact "Date" (*Tarikh*) of the month of **Tir**, as calculated through the movements of the winds and the planets. Furthermore, the "Splendor of Jupiter" (*Ijya-dyuti*)—the one whose brilliance is like Jupiter <note>original: *Ijya*; a common name for the planet Jupiter, associated with wisdom and royal priests</note>—resides in a position of great valor. 

One might argue that previously, the title "Universal Sovereign" was applied generally, but here it is shown to be even more significant because of this specific, miraculous military achievement. By describing the capture of the army/fortress in detail, the author demonstrates the King's profound knowledge, which is like an ocean of wisdom.

<summary>This page commemorates the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan’s conquest of Balkh in 1646, using it to bridge Sanskrit astronomical calculations with Persian (Yavana) calendrical systems. The author uses a "word-number" chronogram to record the Hijri year 1055 and the month of Tir, framing the military victory as a manifestation of planetary influence and mathematical precision.</summary>

<keywords>Shah Jahan, Balkh campaign, Hijri era, Persian calendar, Tir month, chronogram, Muniśvara, Yavana, Jupiter, Universal Sovereign</keywords>

...The Earth-bearer: the fourteen jewels and so on. He <unclear>enjoys</unclear> these. 2. This means he enjoys them. Such are the gods who are the objects of desire.
Where the <term>sine of amplitude</term> <note>original: *digjyā*; the sine of the angular distance of a celestial body from the East or West point</note> is found... from the sine of amplitude 3. For the Dvapara age, the multiplication of the desired position... those gods were struck... such a force...
The meaning is that the superiority of this over the rulers of the whole earth is established. Here, beginning with the month of Jamadi <note>original: *jamādila*; likely referring to the Islamic month Jumada al-awwal</note>, the force is multiplied by the sine of amplitude.
Whatever is desired by the heart, that is shown. Among the twelve lunar months, beginning with the month of Mohra <note>original: *mohra*; likely referring to Muharram, the first month of the Islamic calendar</note>, the fifth month is the one called Jamadi-al-Awwal. At that time, it is decorated with twenty-eight... by the sign of the sine of amplitude at that time... by the sign of the "man" <note>original: *nṛ-puṣya-saṃketa*; possibly a technical unit or mnemonic</note> it is multiplied.
Because it consists of a pair, it is the number 2. And also, of those twelve months, the era of Mohra is the top of the year. Thus, the quantity is known. At the end of the month, the desired part is known. The part of knowledge is the sine of amplitude of knowledge... through the one... in one's own day.
And this was said by the astronomer **Jñānarāja** <note>a 16th-century astronomer whose work, the *Siddhāntasundara*, is a primary source for this text</note>: "I am the twelve-fold knowledge" 241. The sun of the sine of amplitude, the two oceans... are struck together.
241. Having removed the square of the <term>expressed radius</term> <note>original: *vyakta-trijyā*; the sine of the radius used in standard calculations</note>, the result is called the "end of the known and unknown." This much is the square of the difference of the unknown...
Added to the square of the beginning... 244. By taking the radius as the expressed value, the square root of the beginning is subtracted from its own square, which is more than the end and less than the start. By that, the difference between the north and south... it is less than the top. Section 245.

In the **Siddhāntasundara**, the astronomer Jñānarāja has said: "This is not the measurement of the error of the rotation of the mind as stated by others, but this is spoken by us." 247. 3. From the place of the diameter to the place of the sine.
In this way, according to the method of the <term>sine of declination</term> <note>original: *krāntijyā*; the sine of the distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</note> of the planet, from the sine of the degrees of that arc, the degrees of the arc are here 3... the position of the planet. 248.
The mathematical proof (*upapatti*) here is: In the middle of the "seed" <note>original: *bīja*; often refers to algebraic corrections or the core of a calculation</note>, let the denominator of the middle color be understood. # Siddhānta Sārvabhauma Page 21

### Calculation of the Gnomon and Trigonometric Unknowns

The square root of the square of the radius <term>Trijyā</term> <note>The radius of the standard circle used in Indian trigonometry, often 3438 or 120 units</note> minus the square of the known sine of zenith distance <term>Dṛgjyā</term> <note>The sine of the angular distance from the point directly overhead</note> is determined. Then, after performing the 250-fold purification of the previous roots—this is understood by the wise. It is not merely a calculation of the radius, but from that very source—251—just as the measure of the sine of zenith distance is established. 

Because of the stability of the radius, the radius acts as the divider. Then what is the sine of zenith distance? The <term>Bhujā</term> <note>The horizontal side or "arm" of a right-angled triangle</note> is produced. <insert>Difference of the arms</insert> By the refinement of the sine, the gnomon <term>Śaṅku</term> <note>The vertical pillar or sine of altitude</note> is created. Then, also from the equinoctial shadow <term>Palabhā</term> <note>The shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox, used to determine local latitude</note>—where twelve is the perpendicular <term>Koṭi</term>—one calculates what the perpendicular is when the gnomon is the base. In this context, this is the non-drifting base. 

The sines are each brought forth. They are the known arms of the triangle. The known mathematics is stated through the unknowns <term>Avyakta</term> <note>Algebraic variables, usually denoted as 'yā' for yāvat-tāvat (the first unknown, like 'x')</note>: <insert>Unknown 1, Absolute 1</insert>. This is the "Desire of Agni" <note>original: *āgnecha*; likely a technical term for a specific angular direction or calculation step</note> situated within the circle. 

The form of the sine is the radius. From its own latitude, the form of the planet's seizure is previously—the clear path of the gnomon must be known. 2. For these two unknowns, the distance of the limit-point is within the range of the zenith distance. When the position is within the lamp-light <note>original: *dīpāntargatatve*; possibly referring to the visibility or the shadow's range</note>, the north-south degrees of the horizon are, in order, the form of the gnomon:
<insert>Unknown 1, Absolute 1, Clear 2</insert> <insert>Absolute Unknown 7</insert>

At the desired location, on the side staying in the southern direction, the north-south degrees of the horizon are, in order, the gnomon:
— <insert>Side: Unknown 1, Absolute 1, Clear 2</insert> <insert>Absolute Unknown 2</insert>

The distance between the zenith and the desired location is its form: <insert>Unknown 1, Absolute 1, Clear 5</insert>. This is the known calculation for the position staying in the south. Because there is no necessity for the tree-like slope <note>original: *nativad-drumā*; a metaphorical description of a calculated curve or parallax</note>, one stays at the zenith and calculates the limit. 

By the inverse method, that which is established is treated as three-fold. The square of the contact of this is: <insert>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1, Absolute Unknown Square 1</insert>
<insert>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1, Quarter 1, Absolute Unknown 2, Clear 1</insert>

The square of the full unknown is added to the square of the radius. This is the placement for the half-reduction. In order, for the square of the zenith distance of the desired place, in the southern part: <insert>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1, Quarter 1, Absolute Unknown 2, Clear 1</insert> <gloss>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1</gloss>

In the northern part:
<insert>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1, Quarter 1, Absolute Unknown 2, Clear 1</insert> 

When the known position of the desired place is in the south, this is the side: <insert>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1, Quarter 1, Absolute Unknown 2, Clear 1</insert> <gloss>Unknown Square 1, Absolute Square 1</gloss>

From the purification of the other, in the term of the unknown, in the first place, the square of the "Yeme" <note>original: *ṛ-varga*; possibly a specific constant or coefficient</note> is a square with one added. In the second place, the multiplication of the known and unknown is doubled, from which the subtraction is made. In the term of the known, when the known is equal to the radius, the first is positive.

# Siddhānta Sārvabhauma Page 22

### Algebraic Resolutions and the Determination of the Root

When there is an increase, the calculation is performed; then, for the purpose of grasping the root, the <term>yāvattāvat</term> <note>The standard term in Indian mathematics for an "unknown quantity" or variable, similar to 'x' in modern algebra</note> is used. In the first position, there is the square of the unknown quantity with one added. In the second row, when it is divided, the square of the unknown is doubled. This total, combined with the squares, fulfills the previous requirement.

In the first step, in the first position, is the unknown; in the second position, the result of the division. By this sequence, the square of the root is added to the unknown. The root in the first step is slightly more than the unknown; in the second step, it is less. By the mutual purification of the two roots, the remaining distance is found within the area of <term>Jambūdvīpa</term> <note>The "Rose-apple Island," the name for the known world or the Indian subcontinent in traditional cosmology</note>. In the southern and northern divisions, the measure of the root and the unknown is found. By reversing this, the root is to be understood. 

Thus, in the second step, the root in the first position is the measure of the <gloss>calculated</gloss> value. When the two roots are joined, the measure is determined. By reversing the process, the root is joined with the unknown, resulting in a twofold measure; more than this is not stated here, as it falls within the subject of the geography of the inhabited world.

### Geographical Problems and the City of Varanasi

Regarding the region of the banks of the **Godāvari** river, tell me the direction of the sun for one who has reached the path of Hari <note>Likely referring to a specific pilgrimage site or a mathematical horizon problem</note>. One part is reached by the feet... the direction of Shiva... 

...Tell me likewise the latitude of the desired line. **Varanasi** 252. The wisdom of the lineage... the heart... Varanasi... 253. Thus, this is established.

### Final Colophon

This concludes the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma* (The Universal Doctrine of Astronomy), composed by the lordly **Muniśvara**. This completes the first half of the work.

The first half was imagined for the purpose of understanding the globe and the motions of the spheres. This wheel of the stars was created to be understood by those who possess true knowledge. 

1. This work was composed by the grace of the sage **Muniśvara**, who is a master of the supreme Brahma of light (the science of Jyotiṣa). He is the creator of fine poetry. 

2. This work was completed in the month of **Bhadrapada**, during the bright fortnight, on the second lunar day, a Monday, under the asterism of **Puṣya**. 

3. In the year **1568** of the **Shaka** era <note>Approximately 1646 CE</note>, the great sage Muniśvara completed this *Sārvabhauma* ("Universal") treatise. 

4. Let the scholars behold this *Sārvabhauma*. By merely looking at it, the most difficult meanings are revealed. It is a lamp that destroys the darkness of all previous astronomical doctrines and fulfills all desires. 

5. I, the sage Muniśvara, have offered this work with devotion at the lotus feet of **Jagannātha** (the Lord of the Universe). May He be pleased with me, who considers this science of light to be the foremost of all branches of knowledge.

Thus ends the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma*, composed by the astronomer **Muniśvara**, also known by the name **Viśvarūpa**, who is the son of **Raṅganātha**, the crown-gem of all calculators.

The second half of the book has been reached. 
Number of verses in the second half: 100. 
Total number of verses in the complete book: 2400. 
Date of this copy: **Samvat 1938** <note>Approximately 1881 CE</note>, month of Ashadha.

<summary>This page concludes the Siddhānta Sārvabhauma, detailing algebraic methods for solving astronomical unknowns and providing the formal colophon which dates the original composition to 1646 CE and the current transcription to 1881 CE.</summary>

# Si. Sā. Shi.
## Page 22

**Written by the Priest (Purohit) Sadhu Ram. For the sake of auspiciousness and well-being:**

What is that quantity <note>original: "rāśiḥ"; refers to an unknown numerical value or variable</note> which, when multiplied by five, added to its own cube, and then multiplied by four <note>The text describes a cubic equation common in Indian algebraic tradition</note>, becomes equal to the number of Shiva <note>In the Katapayadi system and traditional numerology, "Shiva" represents the number 11</note>? 

What is that quantity which, when multiplied by two hundred and added to the square of that quantity, with twice the square of the quantity subtracted, equals the value of the Vedas (4), the Mountains (7), the Moon (1), and the Moon (1) <note>original: "veda adri śaśi candra"; reading the digits from right to left according to the "Ankanam Vamato Gati" rule, this represents the number 1174</note>?

---

It is hereby certified that this book, the *Hindu Siddhānta Sārvabhauma* <note>The "Universal System of Hindu Astronomy"</note>, was transcribed by the scribe Sadhu Ram. It was originally copied for the Shri Raghunath Ji Library from the Ranbir Library in the year 1958 of the Bikrami era <note>approx. 1901 CE</note>. The total verse count is approximately 700. 

This record was written on May 23, 1928. This book was subsequently placed in the Library for the Protection of the Hindu Religion on February 30 <note>This date is likely a clerical error in the original Urdu text</note>, 1931.

<insert>
Administrator, Shri Raghunath Temple,
Jammu.
</insert>

->JAMMU<-

---

**No.** 3053 <note>original: ३०५३</note>

**Title** <term>The Universal System of Astronomy</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Siddhānta Sārvabhaumaḥ*. This is a significant 17th-century treatise on mathematical astronomy composed by the scholar Munīśvara. The title suggests a "sovereign" or "all-encompassing" system of cosmic principles.</note>

**Author** <note>The author field is marked with a cross or left blank in this entry, though the work is historically attributed to Munīśvara.</note>

**Extent** 22 <note>original: २२; this indicates the manuscript consists of 22 individual leaves or folios.</note> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Age** <note>This field was left blank by the cataloger.</note>

**Subject** <term>Astronomy</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Jyotiṣaṃ*; specifically the mathematical branch of Indian astronomy used to calculate planetary movements and time.</note>

---

<note>This identification slip is typical of the archival efforts in the late 19th and early 20th centuries to organize the thousands of manuscripts in the Raghunath Temple collection. The label would have been pasted onto a cloth wrapping (bastā) used to protect the palm-leaf or paper folios from the elements.</note>

The term <term>Jyotiṣa</term> is the traditional Sanskrit name for the "science of light," which includes both mathematical astronomy (Siddhānta) and astrology.</note>

# Shri Raghunath Temple Manuscript Library, JAMMU

| | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

<meta>This entry continues the documentation of the Shri Raghunath Temple Manuscript Library’s cataloging system in Jammu. The slip identifies a specific astronomical text within the collection, maintained by the Dharmartha Trust.</meta>

# Shri Raghunath Temple Manuscript Library,
## JAMMU

| | |
|:---|:---|
| **No.** | 3053 <note>original: ३०५३</note> |
| **Title** | <term>The Universal System</term> <note>original: सिद्धांत सार्वभौमः (*Siddhānta Sārvabhaumaḥ*). This is a celebrated 17th-century treatise on mathematical astronomy written by the scholar Munīśvara. The title implies a "universal" or "sovereign" system of astronomical principles.</note> |
| **Author** | <unclear>Not listed</unclear> |
| **Extent** | 22 <note>original: २२. This refers to the number of folios (leaves) in the manuscript.</note> |
| **Age** | <unclear>Not listed</unclear> |
| **Subject** | <term>Astronomy</term> <note>original: ज्योतिष (*Jyotiṣa*). In this context, it refers specifically to *Siddhānta*, the branch of Indian science dealing with mathematical astronomy and the calculation of planetary positions.</note> |
